
# Drew Shannon's Blog Archive

# June 2018

## This Week

**Date:** Jun 03, 2018 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/174538189899/this-week)

"Just because it’s fun to hang out at the water cooler at work, it doesn’t mean I want to work there....

There’s also a subtle side effect to asynchronish business, and that is its effect on the decision making process. When work gets done over email, there’s a general expectation of a response buffer of at least an hour or two. In you, though, people can convene and decide on anything at any time.

This is awesome for speeding up the tempo of company directives, but it also places a ton of pressure on everyone involved to maintain even MORE Slack omnipresence; if any discussion might lead to a decision being made, that provides a whole lot of incentive to be available for as many discussions as possible.

Even worse, those with the least on their plates can maintain the most Slack presence, which leads to the most gregariously unengaged representing the majority of the discussion base while penalizing those who are fully engaged in their “real” work."- [Slack, I’m Breaking Up with You (Samuel Hulick for UserOnboard)](https://ux.useronboard.com/slack-i-m-breaking-up-with-you-54600ace03ea)

“Harmon handled it the way he handles everything: out loud, in front of strangers. It’s the way he handled his divorce (episode 167\) and the complicated relationship he has with his parents (too many episodes to name). It’s how he’s confronted the estranged brother who called him a lazy piece of shit and hasn’t spoken to him in 12 years. It’s where he works out his guilt about his sister, born with the rare neurological disorder Rett syndrome, which has left her institutionalized for most of her life. ‘I feel like I was born a criminal,’ Harmon tells me. ‘With a karmic credit card that’s maxed out.’ He says these things in hope of staving off inevitable retribution.” \- [The Tortured Mind Of Dan Harmon (Sean O’Neal for GQ)](https://www.gq.com/story/dan-harmon-rick-and-morty-profile)

**Tags:** \#this week, \#samuel hulick, \#useronboard, \#gq, \#sean o'neal, \#boz, \#andrew bosworth, \#david segal, \#nytimes, \#new york times, \#the new york times, \#seth godin, \#seth's blog

# August 2017

## This Week

**Date:** Aug 25, 2017 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/164601856655/this-week)

"Intellectually, you have trouble making decisions, and you struggle with circular thinking. You torture yourself with the “rightness” of your choices a lot, probably not just in this realm. And once you decide something, you feel like you have to stick by that decision or you’re weak and bad. You’re also very ethical. You take a lot of responsibility for other people. That’s not the profile of a lifelong commitment-phobe; that’s the profile of someone who’s always in danger of settling too quickly instead of watching and waiting to see how she feels and what she’s dealing with." \- [Ask Polly: Was I Wrong to Dump My Boyfriend? (Heather Havrilesky for The Cut)](https://www.thecut.com/2017/06/ask-polly-was-i-wrong-to-dump-my-boyfriend.html)

“'Medium feels like the perfect Obama-era platform: minimal, distinguished, self-important, elevated,’ Mr. Watson says. ‘But as soon as the campaigns really ramped up their efforts in the primaries, we were living in a post-Obama world, metaphorically, and then literally. And that post-Obama world holds no room for minimalist, distinguished, self-important, elevated thoughtfulness.’” \- [‘The Internet Is Broken’: @ev Is Trying to Salvage It (David Streitfeld for The New York Times)](https://www.nytimes.com/2017/05/20/technology/evan-williams-medium-twitter-internet.html?_r=0)

“Twitter is short-form, real-time, and text-based. It’s built for instant alerts and rapid consumption. It is an ideal system for delivering sips of information from an abundant stream. But the live-video effort forces you not only to leave the stream but to set aside time to watch. This is an idea that must have come from a financial guy’s head: We need to boost engagement and make money, so let’s live-stream and keep people longer and sell advertisements. The question is, does any Twitter user want this?” \- [What’s Wrong with Twitter’s Live-Video Strategy (Om Malik for The New Yorker)](http://www.newyorker.com/business/currency/whats-wrong-with-twitters-live-video-strategy)

“Calling ‘Idiocracy’ a documentary is one of those jokes about Donald Drumpf that was made constantly in the latter months of 2016 and now reeks of a certain strain of ineffectual liberal smugness.” \- [Mike Judge, the Bard of Suck (Willy Staley for The New York Times Magazine)](https://www.nytimes.com/2017/04/13/magazine/mike-judge-the-bard-of-suck.html?nytmobile=0)

“How many better opportunities have you missed because that project made you too busy to pursue them? Being willing to be inactive or less active means you’ll be available when something truly worthy of your best effort comes along. It also means you’ll have the time and space to go looking for those really worthwhile projects. If you’re busy being busy, you’ll miss them.” \- [Being Lazy Is the Key to Success, According to the Best-Selling Author of ‘Moneyball’ (Minda Zetlin for Inc.)](https://www.inc.com/minda-zetlin/why-being-lazy-makes-you-successful-according-to-the-bestselling-author-of-money.html)

**Tags:** \#this week, \#the cut, \#ask polly, \#heather havrilesky, \#gary shteyngart, \#the new yorker, \#the new york times, \#inc, \#Minda Zetlin, \#willy staley, \#om malik, \#the new york times magazine, \#david streitfeld

# September 2016

## This Week

**Date:** Sep 16, 2016 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/150497624804/this-week)

"Like the body itself, a home is something both looked at and lived in, a duality that in neither case I have managed to reconcile. I retain the belief that other people’s homes are real where mine is a fabrication, just as I imagine others to live inner lives less flawed than my own." \- [Making House: Notes on Domesticity (Rachel Cusk for The New York Times)](http://www.nytimes.com/2016/09/04/magazine/making-house-notes-on-domesticity.html)

“It’s the entire Michigan-football experience that captivates, however. The Big House has always been the largest football venue for any team, college or pro, in the country. It holds more than a hundred and seven thousand people—twenty-five thousand more than the largest stadium for a pro team, the MetLife Stadium, shared by the Giants and the Jets in New York.” \- [The Mania of Michigan Football (Robin Wright for The New Yorker)](http://www.newyorker.com/news/sporting-scene/the-mania-of-michigan-football)

# August 2016

## This Week

**Date:** Aug 19, 2016 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/149186586059/this-week)

"Listen, if there isn’t something going off the rails on your team, then I know you are micro-managing them. You are really good at what you do, and if you stay in the weeds on everything, you’ll keep things going perfectly, for a while. But eventually two things will happen. One, you will burn out. And two, you will eventually start to seriously piss off your team. So I better see some things going sideways, on a fairly regular basis." \- [How a single conversation with my boss changed my view on delegation and failure (Margaret Gould Stewart for Medium)](https://shift.newco.co/how-a-single-conversation-with-my-boss-changed-my-view-on-delegation-and-failure-ae5376451c8d#.aztg6dmd1)

# March 2016

## This Week

**Date:** Mar 18, 2016 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/141269615774/this-week)

"Facebook has a heavy meeting culture–at least I know it does on my team–so I try to protect my calendar as much as I can. I will put in blocks of time so that people can’t block me out. If something is important they’ll reach out via email or messenger and I’ll open up a time. I found that helps me stay focused on the things I think I should be focused on. I have a lot of team meetings and product meetings, as is, so protecting my calendar has been a very good exercise and productivity boost for me." \- [Geoff Teehan, Product Design Director at Facebook (Geoff Teehan for Ways We Work)](http://wayswework.io/interviews/geoff-teehan-product-design-director-at-facebook)

## This Week

**Date:** Mar 11, 2016 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/140874620334/this-week)

"For an activity that’s so mundane, email seems to be infused with an extraordinary amount of dread and guilt. Several studies have linked frequent email-checking with higher levels of anxiety. One study found that constant email-checkers also had heart activity that suggested higher levels of cortisol, a hormone associated with stress—until they were banned from their inboxes." \- [The Triumph of Email (Adrienne LaFrance for The Atlantic)](http://www.theatlantic.com/technology/archive/2016/01/what-comes-after-email/422625/)

“Right now, the first meal of the day in Los Angeles is arguably one of the most interesting and satisfying meals, of any sort, in the United States. If the nation has a capital of breakfast R\&D, this could be it.” \- [Los Angeles: City of Breakfast (Jeff Gordinier for The New York Times)](http://www.nytimes.com/2016/02/24/dining/los-angeles-restaurants-breakfast.html)

“Hello. Tell me about you. @jonnysun: I’m @jonnysun\! On Twitter I suppose I write very loosely through the perspective of an alien trying to learn his way around being a human.” \- [What It’s Really Like to Be a Popular ‘Weird Twitter’ Personality (Tom Usher for Vice)](http://www.vice.com/en_uk/read/joke-twitter-celebrities-endhoos-jonnysun-dubstep4dads-keetpotato-ruinedpicnic)

“If you’re cold: Take a bath. If you’re sad: Take a bath. If you’re trying to drink less or eat less or send needy texts less but are so panicked that your shoulders are at your ears and you can’t stop worrying you said a weird thing in an email and your friend is trying to think of a kind way to tell you she never wants to talk to you again: Take a bath.” \- [Baths: The Key to Surviving February (Meaghan O'Connell for The Cut)](http://nymag.com/thecut/2016/02/baths-the-key-to-surviving-february.html)

“Maybe he was so afraid of being a bad person that when he was finally given permission by death, he was allowed the freedom to be himself. I really identify with that. I’m not selling meth. I’m not killing anybody; that’s not Mickey’s agenda. That’s not Gus’s agenda, but there’s something that Mickey does: She knows the difference between right and wrong, and sometimes she’ll make the wrong choice on purpose. Maybe the consequences for her aren’t so black and white. Maybe that’s the truth with Gus. I’m so sick of seeing the nerdy white boy as the hero to the crazy girl. I lived in that fantasy for so long, the Cinderella complex. I think Gus probably has that fantasy, too. I think he really loves the idea of being needed, being able to fix somebody. There’s a lot of anger behind that.” \- [Lesley Arfin Talks Love, On-Screen and Off (Julia Felsenthal for Vogue)](http://www.vogue.com/13403037/lesley-arfin-love-interview/)

**Tags:** \#this week, \#adrienne lafrance, \#the atlantic, \#tom usher, \#vice, \#the new york times, \#jeff gardinier, \#Meaghan O'Connell, \#the cut, \#vogue, \#julia felsenthal, \#lesley arfin, \#Heather Havrilesky, \#tim cook, \#apple.com, \#apple

# February 2016

## This Week

**Date:** Feb 12, 2016 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/139183908559/this-week)

"Cummings has been in Congress since 1996, and he is a firm believer in the power of government to improve industry through regulation. And yet now he was begging the former C.E.O. of a relatively minor pharmaceutical company to ‘change the system’? It seemed like an act of abdication. The Republican-led committee was no more impressive. As if to establish that Turing was unnecessarily profitable, the committee released documents showing that the company had thrown a lavish party—fireworks included—and given some executives six-figure raises. (If this now counts as corporate behavior worthy of oversight and reform, the committee may soon find its schedule overbooked.) And then there was John Mica, a Republican from Florida, who has vowed to ‘keep the government out of patients’ sick beds.’ Notwithstanding his skepticism of government intervention, he expressed alarm that some drug prices have “skyrocketed.” Even more than his colleagues, he seemed taken aback by the star witness’s recalcitrance, as if he couldn’t fathom why a private citizen wouldn’t be more deferential to his government—at one point, he threatened to move to hold Shkreli in contempt." \- [Everyone Hates Martin Shkreli. Everyone Is Missing the Point \- (Kelefa Sanneh for The New Yorker)](http://www.newyorker.com/culture/cultural-comment/everyone-hates-martin-shkreli-everyone-is-missing-the-point)

"There are businesses out there with very deep pockets who view having a shop on that street as strictly brand building. There’s a lot of money to be made right now if you own property on Abbot Kinney." \- [The Rise and Fall of Abbot Kinney, or, How Venice Is Dying (Dan Dunn for Thrillist)](https://www.thrillist.com/lifestyle/los-angeles/the-rise-and-fall-of-abbot-kinney)

"And this is the problem with buying into The Shield, believing that athletes are or can be moral leaders, or thinking that a sheltered and not especially bright rich kid who’s only worked for one employer his entire life is going be the person who makes a difference in our country’s longstanding problem with domestic violence. All of that is just public relations. Expecting the NFL to be the difference-maker on an issue so pervasive, so morbidly commonplace in America that the CDC estimates that one out of four women will be the victim of ‘severe physical violence’ by an intimate partner, is at best incredibly optimistic, and that’s before you realize it’s Roger Goodell who’s in charge. On a more realistic level, it ignores that the NFL really only has has one purpose—making as much money as possible." \- [Getting Mad At Greg Hardy Is The Easy Part (Diana Moskovitz for Deadspin)](http://deadspin.com/getting-mad-at-greg-hardy-is-the-easy-part-1735953891?utm_campaign=socialflow_deadspin_facebook&utm_source=deadspin_facebook&utm_medium=socialflow)

"But this Brooklynning—wherein we take something that was fine or even good, and distill it into something hip or exclusive—is all around. It started with our food. Want just a coffee from Gorilla? No luck, it’s all pour over or cold brew and you better have 10 minutes to wait for it. Ask for a well whiskey at Morgan’s BBQ? You’ll be informed that they serve only American-produced bourbon and ‘what flavor profile would you like’ When you asked for the fucking well. Then it hit music festivals. And now apparently even Stravinsky can be repackaged for the 20-something Brooklynite and no one else." \- [Stop Brooklyning Perfectly Good Stuff (Guelda Voien for The Observer)](http://observer.com/2015/09/stop-brooklyning-perfectly-good-stuff/)

“Which, of course, makes me realize that much of my effort apparently wasn’t effort at all, but just ineffective stress added on top of something to make it feel like I’m doing the best I can.” \- [Relax for the same result (Derek Sivers for Sivers.org)](http://sivers.org/relax)

I would wake at five in the morning and run while the world slept. I would run until I vomited. I couldn’t get enough. I was so excited, so energized, that walking felt like sitting still.” \- [Singleminded (Diana Spechler for The New York Times)](http://opinionator.blogs.nytimes.com/2015/06/26/singleminded)

## This Week

"Socality Barbie has amassed more than 7,000 followers so far, adding more each day. ‘I think it’s because she’s so relatable,’ the photographer says. ‘Either your Instagram photos looks just like her’s or you know at least one person who does.’" \- [Hipster Barbie Is So Much Better at Instagram Than You (Taylor Glascock for Wired)](http://www.wired.com/2015/09/hipster-barbie-much-better-instagram/)

“When the Very British Problems Twitter feed already has its own TV show, do we really need Dismaland?” \- [Dismaland Is a Smug, Clichéd Monument to Banksy’s Dated Agenda (Joe Bish for Vice)](http://www.vice.com/read/dismaland-isnt-what-you-think-it-is-or-what-it-thinks-it-is-292)

“I suspect that the way I feel now, at summer’s end, is about how I’ll feel at the end of my life, assuming I have time and mind enough to reflect: bewildered by how unexpectedly everything turned out, regretful about all the things I didn’t get around to, clutching the handful of friends and funny stories I’ve amassed, and wondering where it all went. And I’ll probably still be evading the same truth I’m evading now: that the life I ended up with, much as I complain about it, was pretty much the one I chose. And my dissatisfactions with it are really with my own character, with my hesitation and timidity.” \- [The Summer That Never Was (Tim Kreider for The New York Times)](http://www.nytimes.com/2015/08/30/opinion/sunday/the-summer-that-never-was.html?_r=0)

“When Sacks died this week at 82, he was memorialized as a brilliant doctor and author — the man immortalized by Robin Williams in the film ‘Awakenings.’ Yet, many tributes gave short shrift to an astonishing fact about a man who seemed so empathetic when trying to puzzle through his patients’ most debilitating neurological afflictions and open about his own depression and drug use: He lived in self-imposed celibacy for more than three decades, only coming out in the past few months. And it didn’t sound all that fun.” \- [The tragic story of Oliver Sacks’s celibacy (Justin Wm. Moyer for The Washington Post)](http://www.washingtonpost.com/news/morning-mix/wp/2015/08/31/the-tragic-story-of-oliver-sackss-celibacy-and-homosexuality/)

“An hour or so up the road, we rolled into the outskirts of Petoskey, Hemingway Country. The writer loved resorts. If he wrote about a place, you know it was one of the “in” places of the moment. In the 1920s, it was Paris. In the 1930s, it was Key West. In his early years, it was this lakefront vacation town. His family had a house nearby, in Horton Bay. Hemlock forests and streams, lumber mills, swamps — boyhood adventure condensed to a perfume. I can’t help but see it through the eyes of Nick Adams, the alter-ego who wandered those dazzling early stories: 'Up in Michigan,' 'The Three Day Blow,' 'Big Two-Hearted River.' When Hemingway first published nearly 90 years ago, the town was scandalized. He’d written real gossip about real people. He was persona non grata. Now, of course, all is forgiven.” \- [A Tour of Lake Michigan, My Inland Sea (Rich Cohen for The New York Times)](http://www.nytimes.com/2015/08/30/travel/lake-michigan-tour.html)

**Tags:** \#this week, \#wired, \#taylor glascock, \#vice, \#joe bish, \#the new york times, \#Tim Kreider, \#justin wm. moyer, \#the washington post, \#rich cohen

## This Week

**Date:** Jul 12, 2015 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/123902577149/this-week)

"We are failing to elevate Mr. Mullen’s seminal invention. It is stagnating in the country of its birth. The Japanese, on the other hand, are master kickflippers. Like a pair of jeans from seventy years ago, they give every nuance perverse amounts of attention before creating their own. They probably look for hidden pockets in the concave of a board that we haven’t even noticed yet, or perform sophisticated toe exercises to improve flick capabilities. The average Japanese kickflip is not average." \- [Jiro Dreams of Kickflips: How Japan Mastered One of America’s Greatest Inventions (Quartersnacks)](http://quartersnacks.com/2015/07/jiro-dreams-of-kickflips-how-japan-mastered-one-of-americas-greatest-inventions/)

“If I like a song (or dislike a song), I have to explain \-- to myself \-- why that feeling exists. If I’m writing about the song in a public forum, I have to explain it to other people. But my explanation is never accurate unless I flatly declare, ‘I like this and I don’t know why’ or ‘I dislike this for reasons that can’t be quantified.’ Every other response is the process of taking an abstract feeling and figuring out how I can fit it into a lexicon that matches whatever I already want to believe. My mind is not my own. And once that realization calcified internally, there is no going back. Once you realize you can’t control how you feel, it’s impossible to believe any of your own opinions.” \- [Chuck Klosterman in I Wear The Black Hat](http://www.amazon.com/Wear-Black-Hat-Grappling-Villains/dp/143918450X/ref=as_li_ss_tl?ie=UTF8&qid=1436722047&sr=8-1&keywords=i+wear+the+black+hat&linkCode=sl1&tag=drewshannon-20&linkId=9bc9bfeb5e99f62269f9c1bde0a75f1b)

There has to be risk involved: Anna Wintour can’t pick a dud without potential damage to her image. To be a curator, you must be placing yourself on the line.” \- [Last Life A DJ Saved My Night (Sam Valenti for Medium)](https://medium.com/@drip/last-life-a-dj-saved-my-night-802b1f7f17a5)

“A couple of years ago, I was getting sent this article, ‘Creative People Say No,’ at least twice a day. The idea was that creative geniuses say ‘no’ to a lot of requests (like, a psychology professor researching processes of creative genius) in order to get their work done, so if you want to be a creative genius, you have to say no a lot so you can get your work done. A bunch of people asked me what I thought about it, and I said, ‘It’s good advice for the rich and famous. Creative people say yes until they have enough work that they can say no.’” \- [How to graciously say no to anyone (Austin Kleon for Austin Kleon)](http://tumblr.austinkleon.com/post/120472862666)

2 of my favorite podcasters, [John Siracusa](http://hypercritical.co/) and [Merlin Mann](http://www.merlinmann.com/), have finally joined forces to create a podcast together. It might not make a ton of sense if you’re not familiar with their previous work, but I’m in nerd heaven. [Reconcilable Differences.](http://www.relay.fm/rd)

"The tidbit that stood out to me regarding Recode is that they had 44 full-time employees — plus a few contractors. That’s not lean and mean. The advantage the internet provides to new publishers is that there’s so little overhead. You can go really far with a really small talented team. 44 employees sounds like Recode was trying to go head-to-head with the Wall Street Journal on the business/tech beat. Rather than start small and grow big organically, they wanted to start big. And so to start big they took on investors, and next thing you know, they had to sell." \- [The Big News Sites Still Rule (John Gruber for Daring Fireball)](http://daringfireball.net/linked/2015/05/28/lefsetz-news)

“Yes, I ended up getting the car and the home. I’ve bought hundreds of pairs of sneakers that put Quincy Smith’s collection to shame. I’ve traveled to all sorts of great places. I love buying stuff for my nieces and nephew. I’ve pretended to be an angel investor… And yes, of course, I’ve donated to causes (I’m not totally selfish)… mostly having to do with education (see [www.cfy.org](http://www.cfy.org)). But these are the 2 things I remember most.” \- [You Sold Your Company, What Did You Buy First? Here’s What I Did. “The New Yorker, in a review of research on this nouveau workplace design, determined that the benefits in building camaraderie simply mask the negative effects on work performance. While employees feel like they’re part of a laid-back, innovative enterprise, the environment ultimately damages workers’ attention spans, productivity, creative thinking, and satisfaction. Furthermore, a sense of privacy boosts job performance, while the opposite can cause feelings of helplessness.” \- [Google got it wrong. The open-office trend is destroying the workplace. (Lindsey Kaufman for Washington Post)](http://www.washingtonpost.com/posteverything/wp/2014/12/30/google-got-it-wrong-the-open-office-trend-is-destroying-the-workplace/)

“I’m into commencement addresses lately. They’re a weird genre at which dark, tortured souls seem to unexpectedly excel. (Sarah Agudo for Medium)](https://medium.com/message/i-m-into-commencement-addresses-lately-they-re-a-weird-genre-at-which-dark-tortured-souls-seem-to-ff3fd55ff40)

“All this has led to a new orthodoxy embraced by the upper-middle-brow, in the form of the Michelin Guide and the San Pellegrino list, and by the high-brow/hipster, in the form of the MAD symposium or Ferran Adria’s El Bulli Foundation: Restaurants, bars and coffee shops around the world have started to follow the same template, from preparation to plating, soundtrack to sous-vide, hand-soap to hanger steak. They have begun to look, taste, sound, and smell more and more the same.” \- [Why Are All ‘Good’ Restaurants Around the World Starting To Look the Same? (Tom Downey for Medium)](https://medium.com/gone/why-are-all-good-restaurants-around-the-world-starting-to-look-the-same-d03f454c137d)

“The siren call for many entrepreneurs isn’t money, it’s freedom. The freedom to chart your own path, the freedom to build what you want with the people you love. Taking money, building a board, and raising rounds takes away that freedom little by little. When you take venture money, you work for your investors, not yourself.” \- [Unicorns vs. Horses (Andrew Wilkinson for Medium)](https://medium.com/@awilkinson/unicorns-vs-horses-f81d8dd61f17)

“Allow me to spare you the rest of the truths. But the short answer is simply that I could not find a good argument to tell Tamar we should wait any longer for us to grab our backpacks and hit the road — celebrate our last 25 years together by turning the page and enjoy a perfectly fine mid life crisis full of bliss and beauty, and leave the door open to serendipity for our next leadership opportunities, once our long list of travels and adventures is exhausted.” \- [‘So When Is It Going to Be Time?’ Now, as Google CFO Pichette to Retire From Search Giant. (Kara Swisher for Re/Code)](http://recode.net/2015/03/10/google-cfo-patrick-pichette-to-retire-from-search-giant/)

This comfort can lead to exchanges that are deeper, sadder, smarter, funnier, or weirder than what usually lands on traditional media." \- [How Podcasts Give Listeners New Ways to Talk About Depression and Mental Health (Whitney Matheson for Slate)](http://www.slate.com/articles/arts/ten_years_in_your_ears/2014/12/podcasting_and_depression_how_marc_maron_dave_holmes_and_other_podcasters.html)

Even worse, you might come in second.” \- [This is not the cheapest but it is worth it. (Sean Woolsey)](http://www.seanwoolsey.com/blog/2014/10/4/the-tyranny-of-lowest-price-seth-godin)

# August 2014

## This Week

**Date:** Aug 29, 2014 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/96100269633/this-week)

"Enter the douche ex machina here to ruin the day: the self-righteous friend complaining via Facebook about people not following the rules, the person who texts you a meme about African children without water, or the journalist/media commentator who needs to find a way to call out "hashtag activism." We took something that by all accounts is a success and found a way to make it terrible.

The worst people aren’t the hashtag activists—they’re the ones sitting behind their computers and typing angry prose of disapproval. You know the type, the ones who point out how unrealistic something is when they’re watching a Seth Rogen movie. We know the ALS Ice Bucket Challenge isn’t perfect, just like we know that Seth Rogen couldn’t possibly launch into the ceiling if he sat on an airbag. But that doesn’t mean we need to frantically wave our hands in the air about how not everyone donated, that we should’ve donated the money we spent on ice instead, or that we’re “wasting” buckets of water on our heads.” \- [The Worst Part of the Ice Bucket Challenge Is the People Criticizing It (Angelina Fanous for Vice)](http://www.vice.com/read/the-worst-part-of-the-ice-bucket-challenge-is-the-people-criticizing-it-822)

"Winning an award is about the least humbling thing in the world, and yet when people receive an Oscar or some other illustrious honor, they often say they feel 'humbled.' Really, what winners feel is immense pride—and immense fear of being seen as prideful—and so they cover for it by saying they feel the exact opposite, humility. No one wants to come across as cocky. Are winners right to fear backlash for expressing a natural emotion at their most exultant moment? New research finds that they are."- [Why Oscar Winners Say They’re ‘Humbled’ (Matthew Hutson for The Atlantic)](http://www.theatlantic.com/health/archive/2014/03/why-oscar-winners-say-they-re-humbled/284148/)

"For some baristas, the most memorable stories come simply from watching their regulars live their lives. 'There was a customer who would come in everyday,' Nieto told me. 'He was a clean cut guy—always in a suit. He asked me one day what was in the back room. I told him, ‘That’s our record room. You can go back there and listen to records while you have your coffee.’ He came in one weekend and just hung our there for hours playing rock-and-roll records. Then he started coming in all the time and hanging out back there just listening to records. I started noticing that he was growing his hair out. To make a long story short, within several months he had long hair and a long beard. That was the most awesome metamorphosis I ever saw.'"- [Tales From Our Baristas (Craig Cavallo for Narratively](http://narrative.ly/caffeinated-city/tales-from-our-baristas/))

## This Week

**Date:** Jul 04, 2014 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/90771444319/this-week)

"The English language is mutable. Meanings change. The same thing could be happening to 'humbled.' If we all understand that “humbled” means 'honored,' it can mean 'honored,' and that’s the way it will be. But 'humbled' is a beautiful concept, and an important concept, one that needs to be preserved. The Eric Cantors and Floyd Mayweathers of the world need to be humbled now and then, by which I mean they need to be reminded of their humanness. There’s a reason the Icarus myth has endured. Men cannot reach the sun. Men belong on the ground. To remove the word 'humbled' is to remove checks on any one person gaining too much power or influence."- [“Humbled” Is the New “Literally” (Liam Mathews for Nerve)](http://www.nerve.com/features/humbled-is-the-new-literally)

"It was after this letter that Burnett realized that busyness of a certain kind—meaning not the work-three-menial-jobs-and-put-your-kids-in-precarious-day-care-by-necessity kind—became a mark of social status, that somewhere in the drudgery of checklists and the crumpled heaps one could detect a hint of glamour. 'My God, people are competing about being busy,' Burnett realized. 'It’s about showing status. That if you’re busy, you’re important. You’re leading a full and worthy life. … As if you don’t get to choose, busyness is just there. I call it the nonchoice choice. Because people really do have a choice.'" \- [You're Not As Busy As You Say You Are (Hanna Rosin for Slate)](http://www.slate.com/articles/double_x/doublex/2014/03/brigid_schulte_s_overwhelmed_and_our_epidemic_of_busyness.html)

"It’s that this is as good as it’s going to get. Isn’t that the key? It doesn’t sound very romantic, but when I saw my apartment, I knew it was much better than anything else. It was maybe 30 percent more than I wanted to pay, but it was 200 percent better than anything else I’d seen. I had to contain the expression on my face, because what I was thinking was, Yes, this is the one. That’s how I felt with Derrence." \- [The Playboy Interview: A Candid Conversation with Gawker's Nick Denton (Jeff Bercovici for Playboy)](http://playboysfw.kinja.com/the-playboy-interview-a-candid-conversation-with-gawke-1527302145?src=longreads)

"The professional stress was compounding the personal hell she was privately enduring. 'I’m fucking drinking myself to sleep every single night. I cannot deal with this stress. I need to get back in control of this, and I need to get the fuck away from it for a second,' Grace recalled of this period." \- [A True Trans Soul Rebel (Steven Hyden for Grantland)](http://grantland.com/features/a-true-trans-soul-rebel/)

"These changes are making me feel a bit threatened and defensive. Instead of a lone weird white kid buying a house in Detroit, now I’m part of a movement. I shop at the Whole Foods, knowing every step into that store is a step away from a brand-new city that could be. And if someone tries to break into my house again I will not hesitate to defend myself and someday my family. Some days I feel caught in a tide I cannot row against, but these are the realities. Maybe I’m feeling a bit like the good people of Detroit must have felt to be counted amongst the citizens of 'Murder City.'" \- [Why I Bought A House In Detroit For $500 (Drew Philip for Buzzfeed)](http://www.buzzfeed.com/drewphilp/why-i-bought-a-house-in-detroit-for-500)

"To say the 26-year-old version of Glass lied is like saying Bernie Madoff committed fraud. In the 1990s, Glass didn’t simply unspool a few exaggerations, like theater performer Mike Daisey did in his report on Apple factories in China for NPR’s 'This American Life.' Glass lied, lied some more, and tossed out more lies to cover up other lies wrapped in additional lies." \- [No second chance for Stephen Glass: The long, strange downfall of a journalistic wunderkind (Adam L. Penenberg for Pando Daily)](http://pando.com/2014/01/27/no-second-chance-for-stephen-glass-the-long-strange-downfall-of-a-journalistic-wunderkind/)

"And remember, rather than working so hard to have stuff, better to work to have leisure, the Greek philosopher Aristotle said, upon which happiness depends." \- 5 reasons why you shouldn’t work too hard \- [(Brigid Schulte for The Washington Post)](http://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/she-the-people/wp/2014/02/21/5-things-you-get-from-working-too-hard/)

"It is easy to see the beginnings of things, and harder to see the ends. "'But Mercer, you run a business. You need to participate online. These are your customers, and this is how they express themselves, and how you know if you're succeeding.' Mae's mind churned through a half-dozed Circle tools she knew would help his business, but Mercer was an underachiever. An underachiever who somehow managed to be smug about it.

'See, that's not true, Mae. It's not true. I know I'm successful if I sell chandeliers. If people order them, then I make them, and they pay me money for them. If they have something to say afterward, they can call me or write me. I mean, all this stuff you're involved in, it's all gossip. It's people talking about each other behind their backs. That's the vast majority of this social media, all these reviews, all these comments. Your tools have elevated gossip, hearsay and conjecture to the level of valid, mainstream communication. And besides that, its fucking dorky.'" \- [I'm currently reading (and loving) Dave Eggers' newest book The Circle](http://www.amazon.com/Circle-Dave-Eggers-ebook/dp/B00EGMQIJ0/ref=sr_1_1?ie=UTF8&qid=1389804074&sr=8-1&keywords=the+circle)

# December 2013

## This Week

"But then, earlier this year, I went to a pop-up shop in West Chelsea and I realized it had gone too far. A dozen or so new and old Maine brands, L. L. Bean included, had set up booths in a giant garage and were selling everything from rugged flannels and earflap hats to saddles and benches made of re-claimed pine planks. It was evocative of Maine, but somehow not. The plaid button-downs and the river-guide shirts were as itchily familiar to me as the smell of a leaf pile in the backyard. But the Edison bulbs? The hand-stitched leather wallets? That wasn’t really what I remembered finding in the general store in my dad’s rural town of Stoneham (population: 250)." \- [Is America Finally Over Its Maine Moment? (If So, Thank God) (Chris Rovzar for Vanity Fair)](http://www.vanityfair.com/online/daily/2013/12/America-maine-moment)

"Bankers: First of all, don't cheer in a movie. It's weird. You can laugh, but no cheering. Second, guffawing while Leo attempts to evade federal indictment doesn't exactly help America's perception of your societal value. Hopefully this kind of euphoria was confined to one boozy, Financial District pre-screening (Paramount gave us popcorn and martinis upon entrance), because it would be a real shame if Martin Scorsese just accidentally inspired the future Jordan Belforts of the world." \- [We Saw "The Wolf of Wall Street" With a Bunch of Wall Street Dudes and It Was Disturbing (Steven Perlberg for Business Insider)](http://www.businessinsider.com/banker-pros-cheer-wolf-of-wall-street-2013-12)

"Spike Jonze explores how we feel in love, both now and in the future, but one thing is certain: It doesn’t depend on showing lots of skin, which may, in the end, be the point. Sexual attraction has little to do with what we wear and occurs mostly in our minds. That means no thongs, no side-boob, nary a crop top to be found. And from a fashion perspective, watching the movie in 2013, that is perhaps the most radical idea of all." \- [Review: The Radically Unsexy Fashions of Her (Stella Bugbee for NYMag)](http://nymag.com/thecut/2013/12/review-the-radically-unsexy-fashions-of-her.html)

In the process, it became normal to assemble and project one’s domestic identity for a much broader (and dimly defined) notion of community. Reflexive photo-mania, once a stereotype that defined tourists, had transformed into a ritual of home." \- [Selfies and the Touristification of Everyday Experience (Rolf Potts for Medium)](https://medium.com/editors-picks/c4787112603c)

"I was looking for GIRLS to use alcohol to say something profound about youth, but the fact is that it actually speaks more to the notion of what it means to be an adult." \- [(Not) Drinking with GIRLS’s Hannah Horvath (Jennifer Cacicio for Punch)](http://punchdrink.com/articles/not-drinking-with-girls-hannah-horvath/)

"'In the early days, I bought into the idea that the Internet would lead to a better world, that the truth was out there and that we didn’t need gatekeepers,' he said. The idea that he and many others embraced was that an unfiltered Internet would create a democratic information utopia. 'Now,' he continued, 'I think it’s more complicated than that.'" \- [A Founder of Twitter Goes Long (Matt Richtel for New York Times)](http://www.nytimes.com/2013/11/10/business/a-founder-of-twitter-goes-long.html?pagewanted=all&_r=0)

"This is everything wrong with tech-startup culture, unreasonable expectations, and workaholism in one job posting, by a company with a massive audience that probably contains a very high percentage of young software developers." \- [Penny Arcade’s Insultingly Horrible Job (Marco Arment for Marco.org)](http://www.marco.org/2013/11/26/penny-arcade-awful-job)

"A watered down, slightly burnt cup of cheap coffee in an even cheaper paper cup with a lid that doesn’t quite fit can be one of life’s most satisfying small victories. Hold that leaky cup in the air like Lady Liberty’s torch as proof that your life is still your own, even during a brutal commute, too early in the morning, to a job you hate that doesn’t pay enough." \- [An Ode to Beautiful Brown Sludge (Lilly O'Donnell for Narratively)](http://narrative.ly/caffeinated-city/an-ode-to-beautiful-brown-sludge/)

**Tags:** \#this week, \#rolf potts, \#medium, \#jennifer cacicio, \#punch, \#evan williams, \#new york times, \#matt richtel, \#marco.org, \#marco arment, \#penny arcade, \#narratively, \#lilly o'donnell, \#coffee

## This Week

"In this space between the built reality and the Cabin Porn imagination, a generation of consumers — be they hipsters, wannabe homesteaders, or simply ordinary people — is trying to come to terms with their own lifestyles and relationships to nature. I think this may perhaps be the reason for why so many people find [Cabin Porn](http://cabinporn.tumblr.com/) so appealing. This deep romanticizing of cabin lifestyles is completely unrealistic, as most other porn, but it still has value. Looking at Cabin Porn, trying to articulate exactly what is essential and desirable at the cabin, we are looking at our lives and societies. In dreaming about an idyllic past, we are also imagining the future." \- [What It Means That Urban Hipsters Like Staring at Pictures of Cabins (The Atlantic)](http://www.theatlantic.com/technology/archive/2012/03/what-it-means-that-urban-hipsters-like-staring-at-pictures-of-cabins/254495/)

## This Week

**Date:** Nov 15, 2013 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/67070880774/this-week)

"And perhaps this is why we also now confuse shiny happy hashtag journalism with actual real (and often harsh) journalism. Bourdain didn't set out to validate Detroit. He set out to tell the truth about it. And the truth is that a whole hell of a lot of it looks like a nuclear reactor melted and the city was abandoned overnight and left for the cockroaches. But that there are also people still living, still surviving despite that. And the vast overwhelming majority of them aren't white yuppie Brooklyn transplants funded by Detroit Venture Partners. There are black people barbecuing (yes, Anthony Bourdain came to Detroit and HUNG OUT WITH BLACK PEOPLE...which might actually be a national television first)." \- [\[EID Rants\] He had you until "Chernobyl:" Bourdain came into town and people lost their shit (Eat It Detroit)](http://www.eatitdetroit.com/2013/11/eid-rants-he-had-you-until-chernobyl.html)

"You probably only have to interrupt someone a couple times a day before they're unable to work on hard problems at all." \- [Good And Bad Procrastination (Paul Graham)](http://www.paulgraham.com/procrastination.html)

"The key to being controversial is that people have to believe in the authenticity of a story that they could never tell. You can be as crazy (or, as Ye puts it, “genius”) as you want to be, but when it all comes out in the wash, the story must make sense. Charlie Sheen is a great example of a “crazy” dude who makes a whole lot of sense. Sheen, unlike Yeezy, doesn’t have to tell us his crazy is a form of genius, because I think most of would gladly offer up the compliment without provocation. Either Kanye West is trying too damn hard or he’s suffering from some sort of cognitive break. Either way, he’s achieving the opposite of controversy at this point." \- [I’m Quitting Kanye (Felicia Megan Gordon)](https://medium.com/editors-picks/4972c4f7b0f8)

"...there’s always this feeling of wishing or hoping that you’ll eventually arrive somewhere. But, I don’t know anybody who’s ever arrived anywhere. Everybody I know with half a brain is always a little bit nervous about how long they’re going to be okay doing what they’re doing." \- [Merlin Mann (The Great Discontent)](http://thegreatdiscontent.com/merlin-mann)

## This Week

"After thumbing through three-hundred-plus pages, it occurs to me that the Kinfolk cookbook is a variation on a single theme: the creation of a life lived in an Anthropologie catalog. It’s the reason why we get lost in blogs and the lives of strangers. We want to be happy, always. We want a life free of storms and sorrow. We want our linens, and bowls, and kitchens with reclaimed wood — and in this way, Kinfolk succeeds, for its America is rarefied and specific, rife with denizens who are preened to dishabille perfection and apply pretty filters to their photos...While escapism looks lovely on paper, in practice it’s difficult and expensive." \- [The Kinfolk Cookbook: Small Gatherings You’ll Likely Never Be Invited To (Felicia C. Sullivan)](https://medium.com/but-does-it-work/3d0422b57f0a)

"Maybe the biggest sin of the GTA games is the cheerful, spiteful way they rub our faces in what video games make us willing to do, in what video games are. "A not-insignificant number of the vehement New York lovers I know — especially the young twentysomethings — are actually pretty unhappy day to day. I picture the prom king’s girlfriend sitting near him at the party, ignored but still kind of proud to be in the room and on his arm — and incredibly defensive should you suggest she break up with him for someone who dotes on her more." \- [Why I’m Glad I Quit New York at Age 24 (New York Magazine)](http://nymag.com/thecut/2013/09/why-im-glad-i-quit-new-york-at-age-24.html)

"It seems coffee with strangers only serves one purpose: to make you into a more perfect connoisseur of self-consciousness. You know how it feels to hold your own self in one hand and drink from it. You know how it feels when it orbits the space between your lips and then runs down the back of your throat. And everything is ten times more palpable, like when they first invented Technicolor." \- [What Happens When You Have Coffee With Strangers (Harris Sockel)](https://medium.com/human-parts/12a6ce676e58)

"The thing about being an unstoppable force is that you can really only enjoy the experience of being one when you have something to bash yourself against. You need to have things trying to stop you so that you can get a better sense of how fast you are going as you smash through them. And whenever I was inside the dinosaur costume, that is the only thing I wanted to do." \- [Menace (Hyperbole and a Half)](http://hyperboleandahalf.blogspot.com/2013/10/menace.html)

"I miss writing. I used to do it a lot and enjoyed spilling the words trapped inside the darkness of my head into glowing pixels on someone else’s screen. Truth be told, since we’re being honest here, you and me, I also enjoyed the attention. Maybe that’s because I couldn’t handle it in real life, the part where people like you and want to be with you and understand you and still like you having understood you." \- [The Search for Meaning in a World of Rampant Success and Overwhelming Misery (THE Lance Arthur)](https://medium.com/the-existential-crisis/c2fdd29c35c1)

"Think about it: in an ad impression-and pageview-driven business, a service that allows users to opt out of the noise and get content delivered directly to them is dangerous. When the common practice for bloggers is to publish first, verify second, the paper trail of Google Reader can be an embarrassment. And when sites do everything they can to hook you and increase the critical “time on site” metric or hit you with retargeting cookies, off-site RSS Readers once again stand in the way." \- [Our Regressive Web (Ryan Holiday)](https://medium.com/future-tech-future-market/7b1a7ddb6ffe)

"It seems to me that most of the millennial generation views shooting film as exciting and pure, like vinyl records or artisanal coffee. But trust me, it’s not: You don’t get sued if you scratch the vinyl, or destroy your reputation if you spill the cappuccino. Shooting with film is like flying at night. You have to completely trust your instruments: Your light meter is your life, and there’s often no second chance." \- [Post-process: why the smartphone camera changed photography forever (The Verge)](http://www.theverge.com/2013/6/20/4377290/post-process-why-the-smartphone-camera-changed-photography-forever)

"I can read back that last sentence and see that, bipolar or not, if I’m under treatment and not actually depressed, what the fuck right do I have to be lonely, unhappy or forlorn? Feelings are not something to which one does or does not have rights." \- [Only The Lonely (Stephen Fry)](http://www.stephenfry.com/2013/06/24/only-the-lonely/#sthash.pGQQLIPZ.dpuf)

# May 2012

## New York \-\> Los Angeles

**Date:** May 31, 2012 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/24170787484/new-york-los-angeles)

I'm not sure how \-- or even when \-- it happened exactly. I think it'd been gnawing at the back of my mind for a long time, and eventually became too obvious to ignore. The idea of leaving New York.

I've lived here for almost 4 years, and even though it's never completely felt like "home", it wasn't until the past few months that I finally realized that this isn't the place for me, at least at this point in my life. Most people wake up in New York City and feel a sense of optimism, invigorated by the possibilities that the city has to offer. I used to feel that optimism, but I'm no longer the guy fresh out of college who thinks that living in a shithole apartment and paying $6 for a beer is fun and exciting.

New York is like a city of large immovable objects that slowly sqeeeeeeeeeeze and crush you. In the physical sense, there are the bodies of water that serve as natural barriers, making Manhattan an island, but more than that are the sticky social/cultural things which are a necessity with such a densely populated metropolitan area, but which also make everything harder than they need to be.

And so, New York, it's been fun, but I'm ready for something new. There are dozens (if not hundreds) of people, things, places, sights, sounds, and smells that I'll miss, but I can't wait for what's next.

**Tags:** \#personal, \#nyc, \#california, \#writing

## Untitled

**Date:** Jan 02, 2012 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/15220148992/im-not-really-one-for-new-years-resolutions-but)

I'm not really one for New Years Resolutions, but I think that I need to go on a significant media diet in 2012\.

Most of my insecurities and anxiety comes from external forces that I can't change, most of which come about as a result of being connected 24/7. As hard as I tried, my life was still consumed with thoughts of "the grass is always greener". In the past, I couldn't help but compare myself to others, and it's left me never feeling satisfied, bitter, and angry.

So here is to 2012, a year when I try to be more thoughtful and thankful.

# August 2011

## Untitled

**Date:** Aug 29, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/9545387018/caitlin-chino-and-i-are-very-lucky-and-grateful)

[Caitlin](http://amileormoreinmyshoes.tumblr.com/), [Chino](http://drewshannon.net/tagged/chino), and I are very lucky and grateful to have had a somewhat uneventful experience with Hurricane Irene on Saturday night.

We live close to Battery Park in "Zone A", so we were forced to evacuate the area as of 5pm on Saturday. We crashed at a friend's apartment, and subsisted on a steady diet of Weather Channel, CNN, and junk food.

We're back in our apartment now, and other than the fact that we don't have hot water (the city shut off steam in our building), considering what so many other people have been through in Virginia, North Carolina, and elsewhere, we're very thankful.

**Tags:** \#nyc, \#photo, \#photography, \#caitlin, \#chino, \#fujifilm finepix x100

# July 2011

## Untitled

**Date:** Jul 31, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/8313028487/caitlin-and-i-ordered-bikes-online-a-few-weeks)

[Caitlin](http://amileormoreinmyshoes.tumblr.com/) and I ordered [bikes](http://www.bikesdirect.com/products/mercier/kilott.htm) online a few weeks ago, and since picking them up from our [local bike shop](http://togabikes.com/) last weekend after they were assembled, we've been having an absolute blast on them.

I've also started riding to work, which is about 8 miles roundtrip, and takes me roughly the same amount of time as taking the subway. Adding in the fact that it's healthier, better for the environment, and cheaper (the bike will essentially pay for itself in 3-4 months), it's been a great investment, and my only regret is not doing it sooner.

## Untitled

**Date:** Jul 27, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/8142115014/lately-ive-been-somewhat-seriously-considering)

Lately I've been (somewhat) seriously considering selling / trading my Canon EF 24-70mm f/2.8L lens in exchange for a compact camera, maybe something like the Fujifilm X100.

It's an incredible lens, but as an amateur photographer who does most of their shooting on the go and for the enjoyment of it, I'm thinking that it would be great to have something small that I can take out on the town, while also getting great images.

I'm not a "gear guy" by any means, but [as I mentioned a few days ago](http://drewshannon.net/post/8085478741/minolta-maxxum-5-sigma-28-105mm-f3-8-5-6-uc-iii), the fact that my DSLR is quite bulky with this lens often results in my leaving my camera at home or in my bag.

If anyone is interested in the Canon lens (preferably from NYC), get in touch.

## Untitled

**Date:** Jul 01, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/7134630738/the-books-are-here-the-books-are-here-since)

The books are here\! The books are here\!

Since finishing my 365 Project [about a month ago](http://drewshannon.net/post/6059577959/one-year-later-photo-of-the-day-project-comes-to-an), I've been working hard to put the photos into a book for friends and family.

Today, the 15 final copies from [Blurb](http://www.blurb.com/) arrived in the mail, and I couldn't be happier with the final product. Someday I would love to spend a little more time making a "nicer" curated book with fewer \[better\] photos, but as a reasonably-priced record of my project to show to people, I'm really happy with how it turned out.

112 pages and approximately 400 photos.

**Tags:** \#photo, \#photography, \#Black and White, \#Canon Rebel XSi, \#Photo of the Day, \#blurb books, \#books

## Untitled

**Date:** Jun 29, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/7045342084/today-i-picked-up-a-developed-roll-of-kodak-portra)

Today I picked up a developed roll of Kodak Portra 400 film that I've shot through my [Minolta Maxxum 5](http://drewshannon/net/tagged/minolta_maxxum_5) over the past 2 weeks or so.

I still shoot digital about 95% of the time, but there's definitely a certain "look" to film that I really enjoy, and there's almost nothing like the slow and deliberate mindset that goes into shooting film.

I ended up being pretty pleased with 21 of the 36 shots I took (a much higher yield percentage-wise than digital), so I'll post more photos tomorrow.

# May 2011

## One Year Later, Photo of the Day Project Comes To An End

**Date:** May 31, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/6059577959/one-year-later-photo-of-the-day-project-comes-to)

One year after [announcing](http://drewshannon.net/post/1137725964/new-blog-feature-photo-of-the-day) and [starting](http://drewshannon.net/post/1137732864/photo-of-the-day-union-square-w-hotel) it, my [*Photo of the Day*](http://drewshannon.net/tagged/photo_of_the_day) project has come to an end.

Throughout my life \-- as I'm sure my parents, siblings, friends, and girlfriend can attest \-- I've had more than a few hobbies that occupied every moment of thought, but that rarely stuck with me for any extended period of time. Here's a select few, not counting those that were too short-lived to remember:

- **music** \- trumpet \- skateboarding \- snowboarding \- photography \- guitar \- drums \- guitar (\#2) \- **photography (\#2)**

All this is to say that, more than photography, this past year has been an exercise in commitment and stick-to-it-ive-ness. I've always imagined myself to be a more creative person than my mostly-nonexistent output reflected (even trying to draw a straight line elicits anxiety in me), but since recently picked up a camera again after a 4-5 year "hiatus", I've finally found an outlet that doesn't seem completely impenetrable or boring to me. Setting aside the benefits that come from seeing noticeable improvement of my photos over the past 365 Days, I think photography has "stuck" with me this time.

I have absolutely no desire or intention of doing this professionally. It's just something fun for me.

**Tags:** \#photography, \#Photo of the Day

## Untitled

**Date:** May 05, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/5235223540/whod-have-thought-that-merely-keeping-computer)

\*\*Who'd have thought that merely keeping computer files organized and operating smoothly/efficiently would be so difficult?

As some people may know, I've only recently (as in, within the past 12 months) gotten re-engaged with photography, so for awhile, I'd been able to safely store all of my RAW photo files on my laptop's internal hard drive without any issues. Using Apple's Time Machine software, I also had automatic backups getting stored to my external hard drive in case of computer failure.

\*However,\*a few months ago, I got to the point where my image library was bigger than my internal hard drive storage capacity, which meant that I needed a larger storing system.

After quite a bit of research, consternation, and risky behavior as a result of not having photo backups for a few weeks, I think I've finally gotten to a place that'll allow me to sleep at night.\*\*:

**\- 2 External Hard Drives:***"Primary*[1TB Western Digital external HDD](http://www.amazon.com/Western-Digital-Elements-Desktop-External/dp/B002QEBMB4/ref=sr_1_2?ie=UTF8&qid=1304652576&sr=8-2)*"**and**"Backup*[1.5TB Western Digital external HDD](http://www.amazon.com/Western-Digital-Elements-Desktop-External/dp/B002QEBMBY/ref=sr_1_3?ie=UTF8&qid=1304652576&sr=8-3)\*",\***each separated into 2 partitions**

- **personal documents and \~85gb of music** stored on MacBook Pro internal hard drive, automatically backed up to *"Primary 1TB Western Digital external HDD, Partition \#1"* using Apple Time Machine

**Tags:** \#photography, \#computer, \#adobe, \#adobe lightroom, \#lightroom

## Crowd-sourced jukebox playlist for office stereo system

**Date:** Nov 19, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1619824743/crowd-sourced-jukebox-playlist-for-office-stereo)

I've often wondered what it'd be like to have a crowd-sourced jukebox playlist for an office stereo system.

Say you work in a office with a shared stereo system that plays music for employees to enjoy throughout the workday. People normally make their own mixtapes, play CDs, plug in their iPods, etc, but what if there was a way for the public to remotely input their own choices into that system from their own computers?

On a smaller scale, what if anyone with a web connection could control what was playing on your computer, using a defined music source (the Spotify or Rdio catalogues, for example, or the computer's iTunes library)?

I think it'd be a cool social experiment. To start with, I would set aside 1 hour of music listening every day to public sourcing. I'm sure there would be people trying to get me to listen to Rick Astley for an hour straight, but with the right queueing system, it could also be a cool way to get introduced to new music.

It could also be an interesting marketing idea ("Help control what the folks at Company X are listening to\!").

**Tags:** \#music

# October 2010

## Quote: Why Do You Spread Ideas?

**Date:** Oct 28, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1426278369/quote-why-do-you-spread-ideas)

I spread your idea because... Ideas spread when people choose to spread them. Here are some reasons why: 1\. I spread your idea because it makes me feel generous. 2\. ...because I feel smart alerting others to what I discovered. 3\. ...because I care about the outcome and want you (the creator of the idea) to succeed. 4\. ...because I have no choice. Every time I use your product, I spread the idea (Hotmail, iPad, a tattoo). 5\. ...because there's a financial benefit directly to me (Amazon affiliates, mlm). 6\. ...because it's funny and laughing alone is no fun. 7\. ...because I'm lonely and sharing an idea solves that problem, at least for a while. 8\. ...because I'm angry and I want to enlist others in my outrage (or in shutting you down). 9\. ...because both my friend and I will benefit if I share the idea (Groupon). 10\. ...because you asked me to, and it's hard to say no to you. 11\. ...because I can use the idea to introduce people to one another, and making a match is both fun in the short run and community-building. 12\. ...because your service works better if all my friends use it (email, Facebook). 13\. ...because if everyone knew this idea, I'd be happier. 14\. ...because your idea says something that I have trouble saying directly (AA, a blog post, a book). 15\. ...because I care about someone and this idea will make them happier or healthier. 16\. ...because it's fun to make another teen snicker about prurient stuff we're not supposed to see. 17\. ...because the tribe needs to know about this if we're going to avoid an external threat. 18\. ...because the tribe needs to know about this if we're going to maintain internal order. 19\. ...because it's my job. 20\. I spread your idea because I'm in awe of your art and the only way I can repay you is to share that art with others. (via [Seth Godin](http://sethgodin.typepad.com/seths_blog/2010/10/ideas-spread-when.html?utm_source=feedburner&utm_medium=feed&utm_campaign=Feed%3A+typepad%2Fsethsmainblog+\(Seth%27s+Blog\)))

**Tags:** \#quote, \#Seth Godin

## Decluttering Our Lives

**Date:** Oct 24, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1395162386/decluttering-our-lives)

It started with the dining room table that we knew would be too big for our new apartment. Then it was my collection of about 150 DVDs, followed by the broken Xbox 360 and the gently-used Keurig coffee maker. By the time we were finished, [Caitlin](http://amileormoreinmyshoes.tumblr.com/) and I had sold, donated, or given away hundreds of dollars worth of electronics and clothes, and it had gotten to the point where we needed convincing that certain items should be kept instead of taken to the Salvation Army, instead of the other way around.

A few months ago, I read David Karp's (founder of Tumblr) blog post called ["Life Improvements"](http://www.davidslog.com/776940526/life-improvements), in which he had outlined a number of different changes he had made to his life (presumably to be healthier and less stressed), including throwing unnecessary things away, going to bed earlier, and cooking more. While it wasn't a direct inspiration per se, it helped to further confirm what we had been thinking about the past few weeks: our lives had become so cluttered with *stuff* that it often became difficult to discern what we really needed.

Possibly spurred by our new \[small\] studio apartment, or the million-miles-an-hour lifestyle of New York City, we've recently come up with the idea of trying to start living a simpler, cheaper, and hopefully, happier, life. A new mantra has emerged: ***Buy Less, Travel More.*** Neither of us are big shoppers, and we've always lived within our means, but do I really need that new pair of shoes to go with the 5-6 other pairs that I wear on a normal basis? Probably not.

What had originally come out of the need to get rid of some stuff that wouldn't fit into our new apartment became an experiment \- let's try buying fewer things that we don't really need, and save our money for a rainy day, or for a fun trip (we went to the Hampton's a few weeks ago and had a blast). We're both extremely fortunate to be getting paid to do what we love to do, but it can be hard enough living in New York City on our two salaries; let's help ease the stresses by trying to save more by cutting out unnecessary expenses.

Maybe this could be a huge failure, but all I can say is that we're pretty excited and inspired for what's to come next. Updates to come\!

**Tags:** \#caitlin

**Date:** Jul 10, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1139655360/from-ad-age-how-big-media-makes-it-easier-to)

I just read a great article on Ad Age about "[How Big Media Makes It Easier to Steal Content Than Buy It](http://adage.com/digitalnext/post?article_id=144849)". As I've mentioned in the past, I'm a firm believer in the idea that if you make it easier for your fans to support you and buy your product, they're much more likely to do so. On the other hand, if you set up restricting DRM, force people to jump through hoops, or require too much personal information, you may have lost the customer and all hopes of acquiring their cash. Meanwhile, they'll head to their favorite torrent site for quick, convenient, and free downloading. Here are some excerpts:

## In defense of advertising on artist websites

**Date:** Jun 05, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1138704964/in-defense-of-advertising-on-artist-websites)

I was browsing Twitter today when I saw people talking about seeing an advertisement on their favorite musician's official website, and after going back and forth with a few folks about the issue, I decided that 140 characters wasn't enough to fully explain my point of view. Here are a few tweets I saw:

I get it, I really do. I understand the gut-level...icky-ness...of going to your favorite band's website and seeing banner ads plastered all over the place. I understand that it feels fundamentally wrong that a genuine, passionate musician would let major corporations place their logo on their official website in exchange for a few bucks. I understand that for some people, it screams "sell-out" or "corporate shill". But really, what's the big deal? Allow me to speak on the side of advertising for a moment. To put it bluntly, **band websites have always sucked**. In the past, if I wanted to see a band's new music video, I was much better off going to their MySpace profile or doing a simple Google search. Maybe the site had tour dates or a few crappy photos, but they were almost never updated and often seemed like little more than a web address that the band owned. Meanwhile, band members all had individual Twitter, Facebook, and MySpace accounts that were updated on a regular basis, and new music videos were premiering on MTV.com, AOL Music, or any number of other sites. Imagine how difficult it could be for a fan to keep track of everything, especially when the band's .com site hadn't been updated in 6 weeks. So, at one point, someone decided that there had to be a better way: "Why should MySpace / MTV / AOL / Buzznet be making money from advertising by showing OUR content?" *(note: here's where things get a little more fuzzy and ambiguous to me. I'm not 100% sold on this point of view, but I'll continue nevertheless)*. It makes so much more sense to bring everything (and everyone) to one destination, the band's official website. Bring fans to the site where they can check out the drummer's Twitter feed, "like" the band on Facebook, buy concert tickets, and upload photos from the show last night, all while communicating with like-minded fans and the band through regular webchats and an active message board. Don't know where to watch the newest music video that premiered last week? The official website will have it the day it premieres. All of this is just another way of saying that the artist .com site has become the new destination of choice for a lot of major label acts. Provide fans with a quality experience in your own controlled environment, and they'll keep coming back (Google "Warner Music Group Cisco EOS" for more on this). **CONTENT IS KING**, and just like the desire to bring all content to one official destination, **advertising is just an extension of the long-term digital strategy of the major record labels**. Not to belabor the point or ramble on forever, but the gist is this: moving forward, the idea of the artist official website is going to be more important than ever. Bands will continue to use traditional media partners for promotion, but the .com site is going to be a major hub where all important content lives and can be shared and talked about. With increased traffic comes increased opportunities from companies looking to partner with musicians. In a world where artists already license their music to television commercials or blockbuster movies, web properties are just the next logical step. Agree/Disagree? Let me know in the comments.

---

## Rdio First Impressions

**Date:** Jun 03, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1138656260/rdio-first-impressions)

[Rdio](http://www.rdio.com/) (pronounced "r-dee-oh"), the new cloud-based music service from the guys who started Skype, launched this week, and I was lucky enough to snag one of the 3-day preview invitations. To give a bit of background, here's the rundown of the new site: \- For $9.99 a month, get unlimited streaming of their catalog \-- which includes thousands of major label artists \-- both online and through their various mobile (Blackberry, iPhone, etc.) applications. \- For $4.99 a month, get unlimited web-only access. Listen to as many songs as you want, but only on Rdio.com \- Using Rdio Desktop, sync your iTunes library with the Rdio catalog, making browsing the music you already own even easier. (This system isn't perfect; out of my 11,000+ songs, they were able to correctly match about 4,800 of them.) \- Download MP3's of the songs you like to your computer for about the same price as a normal iTunes download (about $10 for a full album) 24 hours into my preview account, here are some first impressions: \- Rdio couldn't have come along at a better time. There are rumors of a new cloud-based iTunes service being announced next week to possibly take the place of the now-defunct Lala (RIP), and [Spotify still appears to have serious legal obstacles](http://eu.techcrunch.com/2010/06/03/spotify-already-has-30000-u-s-users-so-why-hasnt-it-launched-there-yet/) standing in the way of a full US launch. For Rdio's sake, I hope they can siphon off some subscribers before either of those behemoths come to life. \- **Whether Rdio is your cup of tea or not depends largely on how you listen to music**. \-If you only listen to music on your internet-connected computer or smartphone, **subscribing is a no-brainer**. For the cost of one album a month, you have unlimited access to millions of songs. Some have complained about the breadth of their catalog, but I can only imagine it growing from here. \-On the other hand, if you tend to listen to music on a traditional iPod classic (like I do), don't have a smartphone, or are still skeptical of cloud computing and prefer to **own** your music, I see Rdio as more of a previewing service. Lala was great because you could sign up for free and listen to any song in their catalog once, which made it perfect for previewing and samplig new music. With Rdio, you can preview all you like with a monthly subscription. **To some, that might be worth the $4.99/$9.99 a month. I'm not sure yet.** \- I'm still undecided on the importance on the iTunes-matching feature. Lala did the same thing (and did it much better) for free. Rdio seems to only match your iTunes library for convenience-sake by putting it all in one place. Otherwise, you'd have to search the entire catalog to find what you wanted. Still, the whole catalog is "yours" anyways so I'm not sure that it'd be a big deal. \- [Spotify](http://www.spotify.com/int/new-user/) is free to join (unless you want a monthly "premium" membership) and offers unlimited ad-supported streams. It hasn't launched in the US yet, but I think**it'll be a major player once it does**. Only time will tell how much of an impact it'll have on Rdio. Has anyone else gotten hands-on with it? What do you think?

# May 2010

## Tips on purging an overflowing CD collection?

**Date:** May 31, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1138608079/tips-on-purging-an-overflowing-cd-collection)

I have a lot of CDs, like….a lot. If I had to place a guess as to how many grace the shelves of my old bedroom in my parents’ house, I’d probably say that it’s somewhere between 500 and 1,000, probably closer to the latter. While I somewhat bemoan the digitization of music as a whole and am still one to appreciate the physical product (hence my love of vinyl records), the truth of the matter is that once music makes the digital leap from a CD to my hard drive, I have no need for shiny plastic disc.

As a result, I have stacks and stacks of jewel cases sitting at home, collecting dust and taking up space. So, to make a long story, short, I want to get rid of them. Gone. Away. Purged.

So my question is this: has anyone else ever done this before? I could always use some extra dough to use towards rent, but wouldn’t be able to even venture a guess as to how much they’re worth. Try as I might, I can’t convince myself that they’re worth what they once were.

Sell them on eBay as a lot and donate some of the proceeds to charity? ? Craigslist? Comments welcome.

## Support is not a four-letter word

**Date:** May 09, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137699395/support-is-not-a-four-letter-word)

As I've said before, every once in awhile, we read something that hits us, that makes us nod our heads in agreement and want to send a virtual "high-five" through the ether. My most recent example of this is a new post on [Music Think Tank](http://www.musicthinktank.com/) by Jeremy Schlosberg called ["The Free Music Mirage"](http://www.musicthinktank.com/blog/the-free-music-mirage.html). Here are some choice bits:

I don't think the article is perfect, and I've read powerful arguments on the other side of things. Hell, I wrote a term paper in college about how illegal downloading was an acceptable and logical response to record label price gouging in the 80's and 90's, so I certainly don't claim to have all the answers.

What struck me most, however, is that it's one of the first cogent articles I've read written by an impartial third-party (Schlosberg, as far as I can tell, doesn't earn a living by selling music) that advocates paying for music. Do I really need Bono or the record label executive telling me that I should pay for music?

"Illegal" downloading isn't going anywhere, and trying to stop it is naive and misguided. However, I am of the firm belief that there has never been a better time to be an independent artist, and I think that artists have an unprecedented amount of tools at their disposal to make a decent living doing what they love.

## (WARNING: Superlatives Ahead) \- the 16 most important pages of my life

**Date:** Apr 10, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137649162/warning-superlatives-ahead-the-16-most)

Yesterday, [I posted](http://www.drewshannon.net/looking-to-remember-an-article-from-college/) about an article I read in college that I was dying to read again. I knew the general theme and the lessons I had gleaned, but couldn't recall any specifics. As a result, I've been spending the last week wracking my brain for details and unsuccessfully stringing words together into Google. I wasn't getting anywhere, so I finally emailed my professor, who knew exactly what I was talking about, and sent me a very nice email back with a PDF of the article, called "The Alumnus and the Grading Game" from Howard Kirschenbaum's book *Wad-ja-get? The Grading Game in American Education.* Here are a few select quotes:

Yes, it's cliched and naive. Some of it could be seen as misguided and more than a little idealistic, and perhaps I'm being melodramatic. Reading it now, having experienced some of the trench warfare of the "real world", it seems cutesy. If I had read this for the first time today, it's possible I would have scoffed at its cloying preciousness. Much of our daily lives requires some sort of grading. We can't exactly say, "well boss, my sales figures were pretty dismal this quarter, but I sure learned a lot and, boy, did I grow as a person\!". Nevertheless, it's not hard to see why this article was so influential to me as a young college student. It was refreshing in its tone, and although it didn't rock our classroom (or its traditional grading system) to the core, it was just what I \-- an aimless college student with no idea of what I wanted to be when I grew up \-- needed. Such a simple and obvious concept.

## Rewarding listening habits through a "musical foursquare"

**Date:** Apr 03, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137631043/rewarding-listening-habits-through-a-musical)

I'm admittedly a bit of an information junkie (perhaps to a fault). When I'm looking to buy my next unnecessary tech toy, splurge for a nice dinner out, or plan a trip, I spend countless hours doing research, whether it's reading customer reviews, combing Youtube for video reviews, or lurking message boards to hear what people have to say. What can I say, I like making an informed decision. I'm generally not a math guy, but in general, I can't get enough information and statistics. When my borderline-obsession with information collides with my biggest passion in life, music, it doesn't get much better than [Last.fm](http://www.last.fm/home). I pour over my weekly stats, and am continually surprised at how my listening habits have changed over the years (the site has tracked nearly every song I've listened to since 2006). Although I'm no longer working with music on a day-to-day basis in the professional sense, I'm still very much interested in the industry, especially on the marketing side of things. As such, I've recently been thinking about Last.fm and how it could potentially be used by the bands and labels. I recently read a fantastic piece by Tom Ewing that discusses this in quite a bit of detail. There's too much in his article for me to completely unpack and discuss right now, but check out the excerpts below. I also highly recommend reading the whole thing [HERE](http://pitchfork.com/features/poptimist/7783-poptimist-27/)

.

I think every marketer, be it of music or otherwise, wishes they could easily communicate with their "superfans". The trendsetters and insiders. The people that proudly shout your name from the rooftops. I had the pleasure of working with [many](http://www.castrocopia.com/) [such](http://www.needtobreathebreathers.com/) [people](http://www.myspace.com/halestormstreetteam) during my time at Atlantic, and it made my job 1,000 times easier. I'd love to see Last.fm partner with a band or label for contests, marketing campaigns, or just for the hell of it. Find a way to reward and encourage those that listen to your new album 100 times the week it comes out. Welcome new fans who have just listened to your music for the first time. Give a special shout-out to fans who have listened to your entire full discography in the past month. The possibilities are endless.
