## Rambling Thoughts on Social Media

**Date:** Mar 22, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137578127/rambling-thoughts-on-social-media)

Every once in a while, we come across something that makes us say "a-ha\!"; a song, movie, book, article, photograph, or speech that is able to perfectly articulate how we feel. I've been writing notes for this blog post for over a month (as well as mulling things over in my head for much longer), but it was one of those "someone else gets it\!" moments this morning that finally convinced me to flesh things out and write about it. I have a love/hate relationship with social media. One the one hand, I'm present and active on a number of different social media platforms, and I love the way that it's changed both my relationships and the way we do business. As someone who has worked in marketing, I'm continually fascinated in and delighted by the new ways that companies are using social media to reach and communicate with their customers. Social media has no doubt helped bridge the gap between seller & buyer, artist & fan, and celebrity & "normal person", and at least in the theoretical sense, I believe that everyone can have an equal voice. Something, however, has been bugging me lately. In his 2009 article "[The Social Media Echo Chamber Makes Me Not Want to Listen](http://www.socialmediaexplorer.com/2009/02/03/the-social-media-echo-chamber-makes-me-not-want-to-listen/)", [Kevin Palmer](http://socialmediaanswers.com/blog) said:

Thank you Kevin\! I've casually talked with people about this, but haven't come across many people discussing the "social media expert" / "personal branding" phenomenon in a serious way. My biggest issue that social media has created a cottage industry that produces no tangible products, and that everyone is happily gulping down the Kool-Aid. Search for "social media expert", and you'll see people hive-mindingly focused on growing friends, hits, followers, linkbacks, things which have no inherent value. I think owe my brother a few million dollars by now as a consequence of our betting on our pickup basketball games, but I'm not sure that really means anything. Just because I can play 4 or 5 guitar chords, that doesn't make me a professional musician, and you'd never see someone call themselves a "brain surgery guru". At best, a lot of this is harmless eEgo-boosting behavior, which, given the disconnect between many of our online and offline lives, is common and widespread. At worst, however, part of it feels like trickery and cheating to me. I don't define myself by my online "persona", but I think a lot of people do define themselves that way. For a lot of people, band with 10,000 MySpace friends \< band with 15,000 friends. On Twitter, people with 50,000 followers are thought to be better, more interesting / influential / successful than those with 15,000, no matter where those come from or how authentic they are. I'm not sure what caused me to think about all of this more than is normal/healthy because they're really just stupid internet profiles and I have no room to judge anyone. Without people taking chances and doing something new, our world would be a vastly different, more boring, place. I'm all for entrepreneurs and originality. In fact, I suppose that some of my criticism could be perceived as jealousy. I guess my thoughts are a little torn and all over the place after all.

## What Do You Think: Should Radio Stations Pay For Performance Rights?

**Date:** Mar 11, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137608084/what-do-you-think-should-radio-stations-pay-for)

Daniel Indiviglio from *The Atlantic*just posted\*\*an a column called, "[Should Radio Stations Pay For Performance Rights](http://www.theatlantic.com/business/archive/2010/03/should-radio-stations-pay-for-performance-rights/37329/)?", about a proposed piece of legislation that would require broadcast radio stations to start paying royalties to musical performers every time their song is played, and the debate is quickly heating up. Although terrestrial (AM/FM) radio stations have long paid royalties to the copyright holders, the idea that *performers* should also be paid, is a new one. Whether or not the proposed tax is good or bad for the radio stations, labels, and artists is up for debate, the crux of the debate hinges on the following:

This is no doubt a complicated issue, and I can hardly wrap my brain around all the legalese. One the one hand, online and satellite radio stations are already paying performance royalties (a move that almost put Pandora out of business before lower rates were negotiated), which compensates session musicians, among others. Why not make broadcast radio do the same? Understandably, artists are learning to find new ways to be compensated for their work. On the other hand, could this potentially put radio stations out of business or force them to change to talk formats, accelerating the movement of music out of the public consciousness? Radio has long been one of the best promotional tools for the music business, and looking at things now, it's hard to believe that there was once a time when the music business paid radio stations to play their music (payola anyone?). Other articles on the subject Forbes \- [Radio Royalties Fight Heats Up In Washington](http://www.forbes.com/2010/01/08/radio-internet-royalties-business-beltway-radio.html) New York Times \- [Dispute Heats Up Over Proposed New Fees for Playing Songs on the Radio](http://www.nytimes.com/2010/03/08/business/media/08royalty.html) **What do you think? Should traditional radio stations be forced to pay royalties to music performers for playing their songs on the air? Let me know in the comments.**

## Elitism vs. Populism: My Thoughts on the Oscars and Grammys

**Date:** Mar 07, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137597403/elitism-vs-populism-my-thoughts-on-the-oscars)

Despite all their self-congratulatory pageantry, I generally enjoy watching award shows, and while my tastes don't always align with the nominees and winners, I can appreciate any event that takes the time to celebrate the movies, music, or TV shows that have elevated their field in the past year. While my girlfriend and I watched the end of [The Oscars](http://oscar.go.com/) last night (we had spent most of the evening at the fantastic New York stop of [Copeland's](http://www.myspace.com/copeland) farewell tour), it struck me how so dissimilar it is to [Music's Biggest Night](http://www.grammy.com/). I don't necessarily think that one event is "better" than the other, but the main difference can be summed up in two words: Elitism and Populism.

Both the Oscars and Grammys display a disconnect \-- albeit opposing ones \-- and part of me wonders if each show could be improved by trying to bridge such gaps. Some detractors of the Oscars claim that it represents elitist Hollywood tastes, and I agree. Winners tend to be picked from the same genres every year (The Best Picture prize has never been given to a film noir, science fiction or an animated film), and nominees are often those of a high-brow variety. In other words, there seems to be a fairly large disconnect from what is nominated and what the average Joe sees at his local theater on Friday night, Avatar being the obvious exception. Last night's Best Picture Winner, The Hurt Locker, wasn't even among the [Top 100 Grossing Movies of 2009](http://boxofficemojo.com/yearly/chart/?yr=2009).

The Grammys, on the other hand, are a largely egalitarian affair. Most nominees have ascended to A-list celebrity status and sell out tours around the world. Taylor Swift's \*Fearless,\*which won Album of the Year at this year's Grammy Awards, was also the [\#1 selling album of 2009](http://www.billboard.com/news/taylor-swift-edges-susan-boyle-for-2009-1004057203.story#/news/taylor-swift-edges-susan-boyle-for-2009-1004057203.story). The other nominees (Black Eyed Peas, Dave Matthews Band, Beyonce, and Lady Gaga) have also sold large numbers as well.

All-in-all, I think the Grammys represent the tastes of the majority, and other than the awards given out in the non-televised ceremony, most of the major nominees are household names. All that's not to say that The Grammys are better because they have a populist sensibility. In fact, [this year's Oscars drew in 15 million more viewers than the Grammys](http://www.variety.com/article/VR1118016200.html?categoryid=14&cs=1&ref=bd_tv), and there's nothing that says that the "best" things are also supposed to be the most popular (or vice versa). Critical darlings often take the top spots, voted on by the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Science, who presumably know that they're talking about when it comes to film.

Isn't that supposed to happen, that the highest regarded films are also the best?

**Tags:** \#music, \#movies, \#music industry, \#oscars, \#grammys

---

## Is copyright litigation getting f^$\#@\*g out of control?

**Date:** Mar 03, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137594350/is-copyright-litigation-getting-f-g-out-of)

\*\*\*Preface:\*\*\**This is a bit like Part II of my*["Is the music industry too busy getting in its own way to move forward?"](http://www.drewshannon.net/is-the-music-industry-too-busy-getting-in-its-own-way-to-move-forward/)*post from a week or so ago, yet is something I really had no intention of writing. In fact, it may not even be necessary because it's really just a link dump of a bunch of news stories I've been reading lately. Nevertheless, here it is:* \-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- Protecting copyrights is important. I think creators of original works have a completely reasonable and understandable desire to protect that work, especially in cases of piracy, [which I'm against](http://www.drewshannon.net/is-the-music-industry-too-busy-getting-in-its-own-way-to-move-forward/). In almost all instances, I'll err on the side of protecting an artist's wishes over just about anything else. When I was younger, I was first in line to denounce Metallica for suing Napster. Years later, I think that they had every right to be upset about their music being spread freely around the web. However, I feel like things are starting to get a little out of control. Almost every day, I read a new story about Company X suing Person Y over what seems like the most insignificant "infringement". I think it could be argued that a lot of these lawsuits are doing more harm than good. As Lawrence Lessig said on a recent appearance on the Colbert Report:

Perhaps it's high time we rethink copyright law. Consider the following news stories and blog posts from the past few weeks: [Japanese Collection Society Wants To Charge You For Tweeting Lyrics](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100303/0414578388.shtml) [Would 2010 Steve Jobs Sue 1996 (Or 1984\) Steve Jobs Over Patents?](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100302/1303598369.shtml) [Copyright Law vs. Art As A Shared Experience](http://www.hypebot.com/hypebot/2010/02/copyright-law-art-as-a-shared-experience.html) [Cute baby video wins battle against music label](http://www.reuters.com/article/idUSTRE6200QZ20100301?type=technologyNews) [Colbert Takes On Ridiculous Restrictions Over Talking About The Olympics](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100223/1517128275.shtml) [As EMI Cites Harvey Danger Lipdub As Inducing Infringement, Harvey Danger Singer Says Lipdub Makes Him Incredibly Happy](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100127/1524037949.shtml) With all of these lawsuits, I can't help but think we're living in a Twilight Zone alternate universe where [The Onion](http://www.theonion.com/content/index) headlines are taking over. Does Prince really think that a simple home video threatens his rights and bank account? I could go on and on about each of the articles, pulling out quotes and explaining why the lawsuits seem frivolous and counter-productive to me, but I won't. People much smarter and more eloquent than I have gone on ad nauseam, and I mostly agree. That's not to say that these lawsuits are completely without merit. Lawyers are getting paid hundreds of thousands of dollars to protect valuable copyrights while I, with too much time on my hands, lob bricks at "the man". It's easy for me to sit here and say that a record label suing a woman for posting a video of her baby dancing to Prince is bullshit, and I'm not so naive as to think that these issues are as simple as I'm making them. Still, I can't help but think that this is wrong.

# February 2010

## The Race To The Bottom

**Date:** Feb 26, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137329036/the-race-to-the-bottom)

Although [I've been critical of Kevin Smith's recent behavior](http://www.drewshannon.net/the-two-sides-of-hollywood/), I really respect and admire his seemingly anti-Hollywood business strategy. Here it is in a nutshell from [a 2006 blog post](http://silentbobspeaks.com/?m=200607):

While I don't necessarily see this approach catching on in Hollywood any time soon (Avatar anyone?), there are music-industry parallels. This simple approach has been the driving force behind independent record labels for years, but I think that in this new economic landscape, it's as important as ever. Looking to the future, I sense that we'll start seeing a "race to the bottom" in the music business; which label can spend the least while still seeing marginal success? [Kevin Kelly postulates that](http://www.kk.org/thetechnium/archives/2008/03/1000_true_fans.php)

## Is the music industry too busy getting in its own way to move forward?

**Date:** Feb 23, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137323685/is-the-music-industry-too-busy-getting-in-its-own)

I'm an outspoken opponent of music piracy, and I have little doubt that it's played an important (and negative) role in the current state of the music industry. While I've occasionally been guilty of it in the past, I find that in most cases, excuses for piracy are generally guilt-driven maneuvers to make people feel better about themselves. I'm sure there are people who disagree, but in my opinion, if you like it, there's no reason you shouldn't buy it unless explicitly advocated by the band or label. That said, piracy isn't going anywhere, and arguments otherwise are naive and represent an unrealistic view of how the world works. Instead, the music industry needs to refocus their attention on providing fans with interesting, unique products. As a music fan, give me a compelling reason to spend money, and I'll gladly part with my cash. Few companies understand this better than [Topspin](http://www.topspinmedia.com/). According to [Hypebot](http://www.hypebot.com/hypebot/2010/02/topspin-shares-impressive-music-marketing-study.html), Topspin stated in a recent presentation that:

So why isn't this happening more? *My opinion is that today's music business \-- particularly the major labels \-- is too busy getting in its own way to move forward.* Take the following three examples, *all taken from the last two weeks*: OK Go lead singer Damian Kulash recently wrote a New York Times Op-Ed piece criticizing EMI, his band's record label, for disabling YouTube embedding of their new music video:

Muse bassist Chris Wolstenholme slammed the band's label after it was announced that they would stop licensing acts on the company's roster for free streaming services such as Spotify and We 7:

---

## The Two Sides of Hollywood

**Date:** Feb 19, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137317047/the-two-sides-of-hollywood)

I'm not a film buff. I love movies, but I probably couldn't tell the difference between Truffaut and Fellini if my life depended on it. Nevertheless, the two "entertainment" news stories that most captured my interest last week were of the Hollywood variety. Circulating the Internet last week was a moving [Esquire feature about Roger Ebert](http://www.esquire.com/features/roger-ebert-0310), the Chicago Sun Times film critic whose cancer and subsequent surgeries have taken away his ability to eat, drink, or speak. Putting aside the fact that I had [a bout with lymphoma in college](http://laliydbta.blogspot.com/2008/04/its-over.html), I'm not sure why this article hit me as hard as it did, but it's stuck with me since reading it a few days ago. I think part of it is Ebert's grace, which is evident throughout the article.

The term is thrown around a lot, but I think Roger really gets it. He understands himself and the world around him, and while I generally view film critics as pretentious, he's become my new favorite person to follow on [Twitter](http://twitter.com/ebertchicago), with [witty](http://twitter.com/ebertchicago/status/9399123809) and [insightful](http://twitter.com/ebertchicago/status/9389352850) things to say. I never thought Ebert was the coolest guy around and regarded him as the epitome of cheeseball (two thumbs up\!), but this article, coupled with his new online personality has made him decidedly hip. On the other side of the coin is Kevin Smith, who was [kicked off a Southwest Airlines flight](http://silentbobspeaks.com/?p=394) earlier this week for reportedly being "too fat to fly". Kevin Smith is one of the rare people in entertainment whose personality I enjoy more than his work, and his weekly podcast, [SModcast](http://smodcast.com/), is the highlight of my week. I'm also a big fan of his "Evening With..." DVDs, which have been filmed during his famous 4-hour-long Q\&A sessions with fans. It's not that I dislike his movies, but I really like him as a person and find his point of view on things refreshing and anti-Hollywood. That's why, being the young left-leaning New Yorker that I am, I stood at the ready to defend him after the [tweet that was heard 'round the world](http://twitter.com/ThatKevinSmith/status/9079110598), lambasting Southwest Airlines for kicking him off the flight and starting his week-long campaign against the "man". Let it be clear that in no way do I support Southwest's behavior. From the way it sounds, they had no real reason to kick Smith off the plane, and they've handled things poorly. However, it's Kevin Smith's behavior that irritates me the most. In the days since, Smith's Twitter feed has been chock-full of tirades against Southwest and it's all that he's talked about. He recorded a special 1.5 hour episode of SModcast detailing the experience and once again calling out Southwest and how they handled the situation, and also recorded a 24-part (\!\!) series of YouTube clips explaining himself further. In these kinds of stories, I'm usually first in line to take huge corporations to task for shady business practices, but not here. Smith, whose humor often revolves around self-deprecating jokes about his weight, went from the guy who could laugh at himself, to the guy who asked \-- practically *begged* \-- for Southwest to publicly acknowledge that he wasn't "too fat to fly". Kevin Smith can call himself fat, but heaven forbid if someone calls him out on it. Mr. Smith, who is certainly overweight, will never be mistaken for thin, and while I applaud him for saying that his weight doesn't bother him, I think he's naive, and perhaps a bit selfish, to think that it wouldn't (or shouldn't) be a sticking point for someone else, let alone Southwest Airlines, a company in an industry known for consumer-unfriendly regulations and red tape. I'm not saying that I'll never see another Kevin Smith movie again, but his behavior this week has soured my opinion of him a little bit. [David Pell of Forbes seems to agree.](http://blogs.forbes.com/velocity/2010/02/18/kevin-smith-is-too-big-for-one-twitter-account/) One week, two articles, two reversals of opinion. Life can be surprising sometimes.

**Tags:** \#movies

# May 2012

## New York \-\> Los Angeles

**Date:** May 31, 2012 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/24170787484/new-york-los-angeles)

I'm not sure how \-- or even when \-- it happened exactly. I think it'd been gnawing at the back of my mind for a long time, and eventually became too obvious to ignore. The idea of leaving New York.

I've lived here for almost 4 years, and even though it's never completely felt like "home", it wasn't until the past few months that I finally realized that this isn't the place for me, at least at this point in my life. Most people wake up in New York City and feel a sense of optimism, invigorated by the possibilities that the city has to offer. I used to feel that optimism, but I'm no longer the guy fresh out of college who thinks that living in a shithole apartment and paying $6 for a beer is fun and exciting.

New York is like a city of large immovable objects that slowly sqeeeeeeeeeeze and crush you. In the physical sense, there are the bodies of water that serve as natural barriers, making Manhattan an island, but more than that are the sticky social/cultural things which are a necessity with such a densely populated metropolitan area, but which also make everything harder than they need to be.

And so, New York, it's been fun, but I'm ready for something new. There are dozens (if not hundreds) of people, things, places, sights, sounds, and smells that I'll miss, but I can't wait for what's next.

**Tags:** \#personal, \#nyc, \#california, \#writing

## Untitled

**Date:** Jan 02, 2012 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/15220148992/im-not-really-one-for-new-years-resolutions-but)

I'm not really one for New Years Resolutions, but I think that I need to go on a significant media diet in 2012\.

Most of my insecurities and anxiety comes from external forces that I can't change, most of which come about as a result of being connected 24/7. As hard as I tried, my life was still consumed with thoughts of "the grass is always greener". In the past, I couldn't help but compare myself to others, and it's left me never feeling satisfied, bitter, and angry.

So here is to 2012, a year when I try to be more thoughtful and thankful.

# August 2011

## Untitled

**Date:** Aug 29, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/9545387018/caitlin-chino-and-i-are-very-lucky-and-grateful)

[Caitlin](http://amileormoreinmyshoes.tumblr.com/), [Chino](http://drewshannon.net/tagged/chino), and I are very lucky and grateful to have had a somewhat uneventful experience with Hurricane Irene on Saturday night.

We live close to Battery Park in "Zone A", so we were forced to evacuate the area as of 5pm on Saturday. We crashed at a friend's apartment, and subsisted on a steady diet of Weather Channel, CNN, and junk food.

We're back in our apartment now, and other than the fact that we don't have hot water (the city shut off steam in our building), considering what so many other people have been through in Virginia, North Carolina, and elsewhere, we're very thankful.

[Caitlin](http://amileormoreinmyshoes.tumblr.com/) and I ordered [bikes](http://www.bikesdirect.com/products/mercier/kilott.htm) online a few weeks ago, and since picking them up from our [local bike shop](http://togabikes.com/) last weekend after they were assembled, we've been having an absolute blast on them.

I've also started riding to work, which is about 8 miles roundtrip, and takes me roughly the same amount of time as taking the subway. ## Untitled

**Date:** Jul 18, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/7762808155/part-i-of-a-recent-roll-of-kodak-porta-160-film)

Part I of a recent roll of [Kodak Porta 160](http://www.kodak.com/global/en/professional/products/films/portra/160main.jhtml?pq-path=2986) film. The weather has been beautiful and sunny lately, which is just what this film needs.

I'm still fairly new to shooting film, so I think I tend to keep things pretty simple out of fear of wasting a shot, perhaps to a fault.

## Untitled

**Date:** Jun 29, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/7045342084/today-i-picked-up-a-developed-roll-of-kodak-portra)

Today I picked up a developed roll of Kodak Portra 400 film that I've shot through my [Minolta Maxxum 5](http://drewshannon/net/tagged/minolta_maxxum_5) over the past 2 weeks or so.

I still shoot digital about 95% of the time, but there's definitely a certain "look" to film that I really enjoy, and there's almost nothing like the slow and deliberate mindset that goes into shooting film.

I ended up being pretty pleased with 21 of the 36 shots I took (a much higher yield percentage-wise than digital), so I'll post more photos tomorrow.

# May 2011

## One Year Later, Photo of the Day Project Comes To An End

**Date:** May 31, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/6059577959/one-year-later-photo-of-the-day-project-comes-to)

One year after [announcing](http://drewshannon.net/post/1137725964/new-blog-feature-photo-of-the-day) and [starting](http://drewshannon.net/post/1137732864/photo-of-the-day-union-square-w-hotel) it, my [*Photo of the Day*](http://drewshannon.net/tagged/photo_of_the_day) project has come to an end.

Throughout my life \-- as I'm sure my parents, siblings, friends, and girlfriend can attest \-- I've had more than a few hobbies that occupied every moment of thought, but that rarely stuck with me for any extended period of time. Here's a select few, not counting those that were too short-lived to remember:

- **music** \- trumpet \- skateboarding \- snowboarding \- photography \- guitar \- drums \- guitar (\#2) \- **photography (\#2)**

All this is to say that, more than photography, this past year has been an exercise in commitment and stick-to-it-ive-ness. I've always imagined myself to be a more creative person than my mostly-nonexistent output reflected (even trying to draw a straight line elicits anxiety in me), but since recently picked up a camera again after a 4-5 year "hiatus", I've finally found an outlet that doesn't seem completely impenetrable or boring to me. Setting aside the benefits that come from seeing noticeable improvement of my photos over the past 365 Days, I think photography has "stuck" with me this time.

I have absolutely no desire or intention of doing this professionally. It's just something fun for me.

**Tags:** \#photography, \#Photo of the Day

## Untitled

**Date:** May 05, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/5235223540/whod-have-thought-that-merely-keeping-computer)

\*\*Who'd have thought that merely keeping computer files organized and operating smoothly/efficiently would be so difficult?

As some people may know, I've only recently (as in, within the past 12 months) gotten re-engaged with photography, so for awhile, I'd been able to safely store all of my RAW photo files on my laptop's internal hard drive without any issues. Using Apple's Time Machine software, I also had automatic backups getting stored to my external hard drive in case of computer failure.

\*However,\*a few months ago, I got to the point where my image library was bigger than my internal hard drive storage capacity, which meant that I needed a larger storing system.

After quite a bit of research, consternation, and risky behavior as a result of not having photo backups for a few weeks, I think I've finally gotten to a place that'll allow me to sleep at night.\*\*:

**\- 2 External Hard Drives:***"Primary*[1TB Western Digital external HDD](http://www.amazon.com/Western-Digital-Elements-Desktop-External/dp/B002QEBMB4/ref=sr_1_2?ie=UTF8&qid=1304652576&sr=8-2)*"**and**"Backup*[1.5TB Western Digital external HDD](http://www.amazon.com/Western-Digital-Elements-Desktop-External/dp/B002QEBMBY/ref=sr_1_3?ie=UTF8&qid=1304652576&sr=8-3)\*",\***each separated into 2 partitions**

- **personal documents and \~85gb of music** stored on MacBook Pro internal hard drive, automatically backed up to *"Primary 1TB Western Digital external HDD, Partition \#1"* using Apple Time Machine

**Tags:** \#photography, \#computer, \#adobe, \#adobe lightroom, \#lightroom

Lucky for me, they had some old camera gear that I'm looking forward to putting to good use.

- Pentax ME Super 35mm film camera \- Pentax-M 28mm f/3.5 lens \- Pentax-M 50mm f/2.0 lens \- Pentax-M 50mm f/1.7 lens \- Pentax-M 135mm f/2.5 lens \- Off-brand 35-70mm f/3.5-5.6 lens

This is actually my 4th film camera, but after playing around with it for a few minutes, it feels awesome, and I think it'll quickly become my go-to when I'm looking to shoot film.

## Untitled

**Date:** Jan 18, 2011 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/2814235407/alt-press-no-money-mo-problems-why-even)

[Alt Press: No Money, Mo’ Problems: Why even successful bands struggle financially](http://www.altpress.com/features/entry/no_money_mo_problems/)

I think this article does a good job of articulating my feelings when someone says that they download all of their music instead of buying it.

I was lucky enough to raise $345 towards men's prostate/testicular cancer awareness, research, and treatment, and from what I can tell, 2010 was Movember's biggest year ever (nearly early $60 million has been raised, according to the [Movember website](http://ca.movember.com/momoney/)).

So, thank you. Thanks to the friends/family members that donated, the people that offered support and encouragement, my boss for not firing me for having such a disgusting thing on my face, my girlfriend for putting up with this thing, and to all my fellow "Mo Bros" for sticking with it.

Unfortunately, I have to wait until Saturday morning to shave, for tomorrow night is the grand "Gala Parte", where I plan to strut my stuff in the quest for this year's "Man of Movember 2010".

## Crowd-sourced jukebox playlist for office stereo system

**Date:** Nov 19, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1619824743/crowd-sourced-jukebox-playlist-for-office-stereo)

I've often wondered what it'd be like to have a crowd-sourced jukebox playlist for an office stereo system.

Say you work in a office with a shared stereo system that plays music for employees to enjoy throughout the workday. People normally make their own mixtapes, play CDs, plug in their iPods, etc, but what if there was a way for the public to remotely input their own choices into that system from their own computers?

On a smaller scale, what if anyone with a web connection could control what was playing on your computer, using a defined music source (the Spotify or Rdio catalogues, for example, or the computer's iTunes library)?

I think it'd be a cool social experiment. To start with, I would set aside 1 hour of music listening every day to public sourcing. I'm sure there would be people trying to get me to listen to Rick Astley for an hour straight, but with the right queueing system, it could also be a cool way to get introduced to new music.

It could also be an interesting marketing idea ("Help control what the folks at Company X are listening to\!").

**Tags:** \#music

# October 2010

## Quote: Why Do You Spread Ideas?

**Date:** Oct 28, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1426278369/quote-why-do-you-spread-ideas)

I spread your idea because... Ideas spread when people choose to spread them. Here are some reasons why: 1\. I spread your idea because it makes me feel generous. 2\. ...because I feel smart alerting others to what I discovered. 3\. ...because I care about the outcome and want you (the creator of the idea) to succeed. 4\. ...because I have no choice. Every time I use your product, I spread the idea (Hotmail, iPad, a tattoo). 5\. ...because there's a financial benefit directly to me (Amazon affiliates, mlm). 6\. ...because it's funny and laughing alone is no fun. 7\. ...because I'm lonely and sharing an idea solves that problem, at least for a while. 8\. ...because I'm angry and I want to enlist others in my outrage (or in shutting you down). 9\. ...because both my friend and I will benefit if I share the idea (Groupon). 10\. ...because you asked me to, and it's hard to say no to you. 11\. ...because I can use the idea to introduce people to one another, and making a match is both fun in the short run and community-building. 12\. ...because your service works better if all my friends use it (email, Facebook). 13\. ...because if everyone knew this idea, I'd be happier. 14\. ...because your idea says something that I have trouble saying directly (AA, a blog post, a book). 15\. ...because I care about someone and this idea will make them happier or healthier. 16\. ...because it's fun to make another teen snicker about prurient stuff we're not supposed to see. 17\. ...because the tribe needs to know about this if we're going to avoid an external threat. 18\. ...because the tribe needs to know about this if we're going to maintain internal order. 19\. ...because it's my job. 20\. I spread your idea because I'm in awe of your art and the only way I can repay you is to share that art with others. (via [Seth Godin](http://sethgodin.typepad.com/seths_blog/2010/10/ideas-spread-when.html?utm_source=feedburner&utm_medium=feed&utm_campaign=Feed%3A+typepad%2Fsethsmainblog+\(Seth%27s+Blog\)))

**Tags:** \#quote, \#Seth Godin

## Decluttering Our Lives

**Date:** Oct 24, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1395162386/decluttering-our-lives)

It started with the dining room table that we knew would be too big for our new apartment. Then it was my collection of about 150 DVDs, followed by the broken Xbox 360 and the gently-used Keurig coffee maker. By the time we were finished, [Caitlin](http://amileormoreinmyshoes.tumblr.com/) and I had sold, donated, or given away hundreds of dollars worth of electronics and clothes, and it had gotten to the point where we needed convincing that certain items should be kept instead of taken to the Salvation Army, instead of the other way around.

A few months ago, I read David Karp's (founder of Tumblr) blog post called ["Life Improvements"](http://www.davidslog.com/776940526/life-improvements), in which he had outlined a number of different changes he had made to his life (presumably to be healthier and less stressed), including throwing unnecessary things away, going to bed earlier, and cooking more. While it wasn't a direct inspiration per se, it helped to further confirm what we had been thinking about the past few weeks: our lives had become so cluttered with *stuff* that it often became difficult to discern what we really needed.

Possibly spurred by our new \[small\] studio apartment, or the million-miles-an-hour lifestyle of New York City, we've recently come up with the idea of trying to start living a simpler, cheaper, and hopefully, happier, life. A new mantra has emerged: ***Buy Less, Travel More.*** Neither of us are big shoppers, and we've always lived within our means, but do I really need that new pair of shoes to go with the 5-6 other pairs that I wear on a normal basis? Probably not.

What had originally come out of the need to get rid of some stuff that wouldn't fit into our new apartment became an experiment \- let's try buying fewer things that we don't really need, and save our money for a rainy day, or for a fun trip (we went to the Hampton's a few weeks ago and had a blast). We're both extremely fortunate to be getting paid to do what we love to do, but it can be hard enough living in New York City on our two salaries; let's help ease the stresses by trying to save more by cutting out unnecessary expenses.

Maybe this could be a huge failure, but all I can say is that we're pretty excited and inspired for what's to come next. Updates to come\!

**Tags:** \#caitlin

## From Ad Age: "How Big Media Makes It Easier to Steal Content Than Buy It"

**Date:** Jul 10, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1139655360/from-ad-age-how-big-media-makes-it-easier-to)

I just read a great article on Ad Age about "[How Big Media Makes It Easier to Steal Content Than Buy It](http://adage.com/digitalnext/post?article_id=144849)". As I've mentioned in the past, I'm a firm believer in the idea that if you make it easier for your fans to support you and buy your product, they're much more likely to do so. On the other hand, if you set up restricting DRM, force people to jump through hoops, or require too much personal information, you may have lost the customer and all hopes of acquiring their cash. Meanwhile, they'll head to their favorite torrent site for quick, convenient, and free downloading. Here are some excerpts:

Read the full article [HERE](http://adage.com/digitalnext/post?article_id=144849) **Have you ever experienced a time where you wanted to pay for something, but the powers-that-be made it so difficult \-- almost to the point where they made it seem like they didn't want your money-- that it was just easier to download illegally, ? Let me know in the comments\!**

# June 2010

## My Favorite Albums of 2010 (so far)...

**Date:** Jun 30, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1139560513/my-favorite-albums-of-2010-so-far)

As the first half of 2010 draws to a close and I'm sitting here at my parent's house in Michigan preparing for a 4th of July weekend full of beer, BBQ's, boats, fireworks, and parades, I reflect on what music has moved me over the past 6 months. I could spend hours crafting this into a carefully-thought out list of my favorite albums and EP's, along with the "biggest surprises" or "biggest disappointments", or any number of other dissections, but I've decided to just keep it simple and let the music do the talking (maybe I'll save the dissertation for the end of the year). Here's what I've been listening to (in no particular order):

## In defense of advertising on artist websites

**Date:** Jun 05, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1138704964/in-defense-of-advertising-on-artist-websites)

I was browsing Twitter today when I saw people talking about seeing an advertisement on their favorite musician's official website, and after going back and forth with a few folks about the issue, I decided that 140 characters wasn't enough to fully explain my point of view. Here are a few tweets I saw:

I get it, I really do. I understand the gut-level...icky-ness...of going to your favorite band's website and seeing banner ads plastered all over the place. I understand that it feels fundamentally wrong that a genuine, passionate musician would let major corporations place their logo on their official website in exchange for a few bucks. I understand that for some people, it screams "sell-out" or "corporate shill". But really, what's the big deal? Allow me to speak on the side of advertising for a moment. To put it bluntly, **band websites have always sucked**. In the past, if I wanted to see a band's new music video, I was much better off going to their MySpace profile or doing a simple Google search. Maybe the site had tour dates or a few crappy photos, but they were almost never updated and often seemed like little more than a web address that the band owned. Meanwhile, band members all had individual Twitter, Facebook, and MySpace accounts that were updated on a regular basis, and new music videos were premiering on MTV.com, AOL Music, or any number of other sites. Imagine how difficult it could be for a fan to keep track of everything, especially when the band's .com site hadn't been updated in 6 weeks. So, at one point, someone decided that there had to be a better way: "Why should MySpace / MTV / AOL / Buzznet be making money from advertising by showing OUR content?" *(note: here's where things get a little more fuzzy and ambiguous to me. I'm not 100% sold on this point of view, but I'll continue nevertheless)*. It makes so much more sense to bring everything (and everyone) to one destination, the band's official website. Bring fans to the site where they can check out the drummer's Twitter feed, "like" the band on Facebook, buy concert tickets, and upload photos from the show last night, all while communicating with like-minded fans and the band through regular webchats and an active message board. Don't know where to watch the newest music video that premiered last week? The official website will have it the day it premieres. All of this is just another way of saying that the artist .com site has become the new destination of choice for a lot of major label acts. Provide fans with a quality experience in your own controlled environment, and they'll keep coming back (Google "Warner Music Group Cisco EOS" for more on this). **CONTENT IS KING**, and just like the desire to bring all content to one official destination, **advertising is just an extension of the long-term digital strategy of the major record labels**. Not to belabor the point or ramble on forever, but the gist is this: moving forward, the idea of the artist official website is going to be more important than ever. Bands will continue to use traditional media partners for promotion, but the .com site is going to be a major hub where all important content lives and can be shared and talked about. With increased traffic comes increased opportunities from companies looking to partner with musicians. In a world where artists already license their music to television commercials or blockbuster movies, web properties are just the next logical step. Agree/Disagree? Let me know in the comments.

---

## Rdio First Impressions

**Date:** Jun 03, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1138656260/rdio-first-impressions)

[Rdio](http://www.rdio.com/) (pronounced "r-dee-oh"), the new cloud-based music service from the guys who started Skype, launched this week, and I was lucky enough to snag one of the 3-day preview invitations. To give a bit of background, here's the rundown of the new site: \- For $9.99 a month, get unlimited streaming of their catalog \-- which includes thousands of major label artists \-- both online and through their various mobile (Blackberry, iPhone, etc.) applications. \- For $4.99 a month, get unlimited web-only access. Listen to as many songs as you want, but only on Rdio.com \- Using Rdio Desktop, sync your iTunes library with the Rdio catalog, making browsing the music you already own even easier. (This system isn't perfect; out of my 11,000+ songs, they were able to correctly match about 4,800 of them.) \- Download MP3's of the songs you like to your computer for about the same price as a normal iTunes download (about $10 for a full album) 24 hours into my preview account, here are some first impressions: \- Rdio couldn't have come along at a better time. There are rumors of a new cloud-based iTunes service being announced next week to possibly take the place of the now-defunct Lala (RIP), and [Spotify still appears to have serious legal obstacles](http://eu.techcrunch.com/2010/06/03/spotify-already-has-30000-u-s-users-so-why-hasnt-it-launched-there-yet/) standing in the way of a full US launch. For Rdio's sake, I hope they can siphon off some subscribers before either of those behemoths come to life. \- **Whether Rdio is your cup of tea or not depends largely on how you listen to music**. \-If you only listen to music on your internet-connected computer or smartphone, **subscribing is a no-brainer**. For the cost of one album a month, you have unlimited access to millions of songs. Some have complained about the breadth of their catalog, but I can only imagine it growing from here. \-On the other hand, if you tend to listen to music on a traditional iPod classic (like I do), don't have a smartphone, or are still skeptical of cloud computing and prefer to **own** your music, I see Rdio as more of a previewing service. Lala was great because you could sign up for free and listen to any song in their catalog once, which made it perfect for previewing and samplig new music. With Rdio, you can preview all you like with a monthly subscription. **To some, that might be worth the $4.99/$9.99 a month. I'm not sure yet.** \- I'm still undecided on the importance on the iTunes-matching feature. Lala did the same thing (and did it much better) for free. Rdio seems to only match your iTunes library for convenience-sake by putting it all in one place. Otherwise, you'd have to search the entire catalog to find what you wanted. Still, the whole catalog is "yours" anyways so I'm not sure that it'd be a big deal. \- [Spotify](http://www.spotify.com/int/new-user/) is free to join (unless you want a monthly "premium" membership) and offers unlimited ad-supported streams. It hasn't launched in the US yet, but I think**it'll be a major player once it does**. Only time will tell how much of an impact it'll have on Rdio. Has anyone else gotten hands-on with it? What do you think?

# May 2010

## Tips on purging an overflowing CD collection?

**Date:** May 31, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1138608079/tips-on-purging-an-overflowing-cd-collection)

I have a lot of CDs, like….a lot. If I had to place a guess as to how many grace the shelves of my old bedroom in my parents’ house, I’d probably say that it’s somewhere between 500 and 1,000, probably closer to the latter. While I somewhat bemoan the digitization of music as a whole and am still one to appreciate the physical product (hence my love of vinyl records), the truth of the matter is that once music makes the digital leap from a CD to my hard drive, I have no need for shiny plastic disc.

As a result, I have stacks and stacks of jewel cases sitting at home, collecting dust and taking up space. So, to make a long story, short, I want to get rid of them. Gone. Away. Purged.

So my question is this: has anyone else ever done this before? I could always use some extra dough to use towards rent, but wouldn’t be able to even venture a guess as to how much they’re worth. Try as I might, I can’t convince myself that they’re worth what they once were.

Sell them on eBay as a lot and donate some of the proceeds to charity? ? Craigslist? Comments welcome.

## Support is not a four-letter word

**Date:** May 09, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137699395/support-is-not-a-four-letter-word)

As I've said before, every once in awhile, we read something that hits us, that makes us nod our heads in agreement and want to send a virtual "high-five" through the ether. My most recent example of this is a new post on [Music Think Tank](http://www.musicthinktank.com/) by Jeremy Schlosberg called ["The Free Music Mirage"](http://www.musicthinktank.com/blog/the-free-music-mirage.html). Here are some choice bits:

I don't think the article is perfect, and I've read powerful arguments on the other side of things. Hell, I wrote a term paper in college about how illegal downloading was an acceptable and logical response to record label price gouging in the 80's and 90's, so I certainly don't claim to have all the answers.

What struck me most, however, is that it's one of the first cogent articles I've read written by an impartial third-party (Schlosberg, as far as I can tell, doesn't earn a living by selling music) that advocates paying for music. Do I really need Bono or the record label executive telling me that I should pay for music?

"Illegal" downloading isn't going anywhere, and trying to stop it is naive and misguided. However, I am of the firm belief that there has never been a better time to be an independent artist, and I think that artists have an unprecedented amount of tools at their disposal to make a decent living doing what they love.

## (WARNING: Superlatives Ahead) \- the 16 most important pages of my life

**Date:** Apr 10, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137649162/warning-superlatives-ahead-the-16-most)

Yesterday, [I posted](http://www.drewshannon.net/looking-to-remember-an-article-from-college/) about an article I read in college that I was dying to read again. I knew the general theme and the lessons I had gleaned, but couldn't recall any specifics. As a result, I've been spending the last week wracking my brain for details and unsuccessfully stringing words together into Google. I wasn't getting anywhere, so I finally emailed my professor, who knew exactly what I was talking about, and sent me a very nice email back with a PDF of the article, called "The Alumnus and the Grading Game" from Howard Kirschenbaum's book *Wad-ja-get? The Grading Game in American Education.* Here are a few select quotes:

Yes, it's cliched and naive. Some of it could be seen as misguided and more than a little idealistic, and perhaps I'm being melodramatic. Reading it now, having experienced some of the trench warfare of the "real world", it seems cutesy. If I had read this for the first time today, it's possible I would have scoffed at its cloying preciousness. Much of our daily lives requires some sort of grading. We can't exactly say, "well boss, my sales figures were pretty dismal this quarter, but I sure learned a lot and, boy, did I grow as a person\!". Nevertheless, it's not hard to see why this article was so influential to me as a young college student. It was refreshing in its tone, and although it didn't rock our classroom (or its traditional grading system) to the core, it was just what I \-- an aimless college student with no idea of what I wanted to be when I grew up \-- needed. Such a simple and obvious concept.

## Rewarding listening habits through a "musical foursquare"

**Date:** Apr 03, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137631043/rewarding-listening-habits-through-a-musical)

I'm admittedly a bit of an information junkie (perhaps to a fault). When I'm looking to buy my next unnecessary tech toy, splurge for a nice dinner out, or plan a trip, I spend countless hours doing research, whether it's reading customer reviews, combing Youtube for video reviews, or lurking message boards to hear what people have to say. What can I say, I like making an informed decision. I'm generally not a math guy, but in general, I can't get enough information and statistics. When my borderline-obsession with information collides with my biggest passion in life, music, it doesn't get much better than [Last.fm](http://www.last.fm/home). I pour over my weekly stats, and am continually surprised at how my listening habits have changed over the years (the site has tracked nearly every song I've listened to since 2006). Although I'm no longer working with music on a day-to-day basis in the professional sense, I'm still very much interested in the industry, especially on the marketing side of things. As such, I've recently been thinking about Last.fm and how it could potentially be used by the bands and labels. I recently read a fantastic piece by Tom Ewing that discusses this in quite a bit of detail. There's too much in his article for me to completely unpack and discuss right now, but check out the excerpts below. I also highly recommend reading the whole thing [HERE](http://pitchfork.com/features/poptimist/7783-poptimist-27/)

.

I think every marketer, be it of music or otherwise, wishes they could easily communicate with their "superfans". The trendsetters and insiders. The people that proudly shout your name from the rooftops. I had the pleasure of working with [many](http://www.castrocopia.com/) [such](http://www.needtobreathebreathers.com/) [people](http://www.myspace.com/halestormstreetteam) during my time at Atlantic, and it made my job 1,000 times easier. I'd love to see Last.fm partner with a band or label for contests, marketing campaigns, or just for the hell of it. Find a way to reward and encourage those that listen to your new album 100 times the week it comes out. Welcome new fans who have just listened to your music for the first time. Give a special shout-out to fans who have listened to your entire full discography in the past month. The possibilities are endless.

## Rambling Thoughts on Social Media

**Date:** Mar 22, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137578127/rambling-thoughts-on-social-media)

Every once in a while, we come across something that makes us say "a-ha\!"; a song, movie, book, article, photograph, or speech that is able to perfectly articulate how we feel. I've been writing notes for this blog post for over a month (as well as mulling things over in my head for much longer), but it was one of those "someone else gets it\!" moments this morning that finally convinced me to flesh things out and write about it. I have a love/hate relationship with social media. One the one hand, I'm present and active on a number of different social media platforms, and I love the way that it's changed both my relationships and the way we do business. As someone who has worked in marketing, I'm continually fascinated in and delighted by the new ways that companies are using social media to reach and communicate with their customers. Social media has no doubt helped bridge the gap between seller & buyer, artist & fan, and celebrity & "normal person", and at least in the theoretical sense, I believe that everyone can have an equal voice. Something, however, has been bugging me lately. In his 2009 article "[The Social Media Echo Chamber Makes Me Not Want to Listen](http://www.socialmediaexplorer.com/2009/02/03/the-social-media-echo-chamber-makes-me-not-want-to-listen/)", [Kevin Palmer](http://socialmediaanswers.com/blog) said:

Thank you Kevin\! I've casually talked with people about this, but haven't come across many people discussing the "social media expert" / "personal branding" phenomenon in a serious way. My biggest issue that social media has created a cottage industry that produces no tangible products, and that everyone is happily gulping down the Kool-Aid. Search for "social media expert", and you'll see people hive-mindingly focused on growing friends, hits, followers, linkbacks, things which have no inherent value. I think owe my brother a few million dollars by now as a consequence of our betting on our pickup basketball games, but I'm not sure that really means anything. Just because I can play 4 or 5 guitar chords, that doesn't make me a professional musician, and you'd never see someone call themselves a "brain surgery guru". At best, a lot of this is harmless eEgo-boosting behavior, which, given the disconnect between many of our online and offline lives, is common and widespread. At worst, however, part of it feels like trickery and cheating to me. I don't define myself by my online "persona", but I think a lot of people do define themselves that way. For a lot of people, band with 10,000 MySpace friends \< band with 15,000 friends. On Twitter, people with 50,000 followers are thought to be better, more interesting / influential / successful than those with 15,000, no matter where those come from or how authentic they are. I'm not sure what caused me to think about all of this more than is normal/healthy because they're really just stupid internet profiles and I have no room to judge anyone. Without people taking chances and doing something new, our world would be a vastly different, more boring, place. I'm all for entrepreneurs and originality. In fact, I suppose that some of my criticism could be perceived as jealousy. I guess my thoughts are a little torn and all over the place after all.

---

## What Do You Think: Should Radio Stations Pay For Performance Rights?

**Date:** Mar 11, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137608084/what-do-you-think-should-radio-stations-pay-for)

Daniel Indiviglio from *The Atlantic*just posted\*\*an a column called, "[Should Radio Stations Pay For Performance Rights](http://www.theatlantic.com/business/archive/2010/03/should-radio-stations-pay-for-performance-rights/37329/)?", about a proposed piece of legislation that would require broadcast radio stations to start paying royalties to musical performers every time their song is played, and the debate is quickly heating up. Although terrestrial (AM/FM) radio stations have long paid royalties to the copyright holders, the idea that *performers* should also be paid, is a new one. Whether or not the proposed tax is good or bad for the radio stations, labels, and artists is up for debate, the crux of the debate hinges on the following:

This is no doubt a complicated issue, and I can hardly wrap my brain around all the legalese. One the one hand, online and satellite radio stations are already paying performance royalties (a move that almost put Pandora out of business before lower rates were negotiated), which compensates session musicians, among others. Why not make broadcast radio do the same? Understandably, artists are learning to find new ways to be compensated for their work. On the other hand, could this potentially put radio stations out of business or force them to change to talk formats, accelerating the movement of music out of the public consciousness? Radio has long been one of the best promotional tools for the music business, and looking at things now, it's hard to believe that there was once a time when the music business paid radio stations to play their music (payola anyone?). Other articles on the subject Forbes \- [Radio Royalties Fight Heats Up In Washington](http://www.forbes.com/2010/01/08/radio-internet-royalties-business-beltway-radio.html) New York Times \- [Dispute Heats Up Over Proposed New Fees for Playing Songs on the Radio](http://www.nytimes.com/2010/03/08/business/media/08royalty.html) **What do you think? Should traditional radio stations be forced to pay royalties to music performers for playing their songs on the air? Let me know in the comments.**

## Elitism vs. Populism: My Thoughts on the Oscars and Grammys

**Date:** Mar 07, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137597403/elitism-vs-populism-my-thoughts-on-the-oscars)

Despite all their self-congratulatory pageantry, I generally enjoy watching award shows, and while my tastes don't always align with the nominees and winners, I can appreciate any event that takes the time to celebrate the movies, music, or TV shows that have elevated their field in the past year. While my girlfriend and I watched the end of [The Oscars](http://oscar.go.com/) last night (we had spent most of the evening at the fantastic New York stop of [Copeland's](http://www.myspace.com/copeland) farewell tour), it struck me how so dissimilar it is to [Music's Biggest Night](http://www.grammy.com/). I don't necessarily think that one event is "better" than the other, but the main difference can be summed up in two words: Elitism and Populism.

Both the Oscars and Grammys display a disconnect \-- albeit opposing ones \-- and part of me wonders if each show could be improved by trying to bridge such gaps. Some detractors of the Oscars claim that it represents elitist Hollywood tastes, and I agree. Winners tend to be picked from the same genres every year (The Best Picture prize has never been given to a film noir, science fiction or an animated film), and nominees are often those of a high-brow variety. In other words, there seems to be a fairly large disconnect from what is nominated and what the average Joe sees at his local theater on Friday night, Avatar being the obvious exception. Last night's Best Picture Winner, The Hurt Locker, wasn't even among the [Top 100 Grossing Movies of 2009](http://boxofficemojo.com/yearly/chart/?yr=2009).

The Grammys, on the other hand, are a largely egalitarian affair. Most nominees have ascended to A-list celebrity status and sell out tours around the world. Taylor Swift's \*Fearless,\*which won Album of the Year at this year's Grammy Awards, was also the [\#1 selling album of 2009](http://www.billboard.com/news/taylor-swift-edges-susan-boyle-for-2009-1004057203.story#/news/taylor-swift-edges-susan-boyle-for-2009-1004057203.story). The other nominees (Black Eyed Peas, Dave Matthews Band, Beyonce, and Lady Gaga) have also sold large numbers as well.

All-in-all, I think the Grammys represent the tastes of the majority, and other than the awards given out in the non-televised ceremony, most of the major nominees are household names. All that's not to say that The Grammys are better because they have a populist sensibility. In fact, [this year's Oscars drew in 15 million more viewers than the Grammys](http://www.variety.com/article/VR1118016200.html?categoryid=14&cs=1&ref=bd_tv), and there's nothing that says that the "best" things are also supposed to be the most popular (or vice versa). Critical darlings often take the top spots, voted on by the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Science, who presumably know that they're talking about when it comes to film.

Isn't that supposed to happen, that the highest regarded films are also the best?

## Is copyright litigation getting f^$\#@\*g out of control?

**Date:** Mar 03, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137594350/is-copyright-litigation-getting-f-g-out-of)

\*\*\*Preface:\*\*\**This is a bit like Part II of my*["Is the music industry too busy getting in its own way to move forward?"](http://www.drewshannon.net/is-the-music-industry-too-busy-getting-in-its-own-way-to-move-forward/)*post from a week or so ago, yet is something I really had no intention of writing. In fact, it may not even be necessary because it's really just a link dump of a bunch of news stories I've been reading lately. Nevertheless, here it is:* \-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- Protecting copyrights is important. I think creators of original works have a completely reasonable and understandable desire to protect that work, especially in cases of piracy, [which I'm against](http://www.drewshannon.net/is-the-music-industry-too-busy-getting-in-its-own-way-to-move-forward/). In almost all instances, I'll err on the side of protecting an artist's wishes over just about anything else. When I was younger, I was first in line to denounce Metallica for suing Napster. Years later, I think that they had every right to be upset about their music being spread freely around the web. However, I feel like things are starting to get a little out of control. Almost every day, I read a new story about Company X suing Person Y over what seems like the most insignificant "infringement". I think it could be argued that a lot of these lawsuits are doing more harm than good. As Lawrence Lessig said on a recent appearance on the Colbert Report:

Perhaps it's high time we rethink copyright law. Consider the following news stories and blog posts from the past few weeks: [Japanese Collection Society Wants To Charge You For Tweeting Lyrics](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100303/0414578388.shtml) [Would 2010 Steve Jobs Sue 1996 (Or 1984\) Steve Jobs Over Patents?](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100302/1303598369.shtml) [Copyright Law vs. Art As A Shared Experience](http://www.hypebot.com/hypebot/2010/02/copyright-law-art-as-a-shared-experience.html) [Cute baby video wins battle against music label](http://www.reuters.com/article/idUSTRE6200QZ20100301?type=technologyNews) [Colbert Takes On Ridiculous Restrictions Over Talking About The Olympics](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100223/1517128275.shtml) [As EMI Cites Harvey Danger Lipdub As Inducing Infringement, Harvey Danger Singer Says Lipdub Makes Him Incredibly Happy](http://techdirt.com/articles/20100127/1524037949.shtml) With all of these lawsuits, I can't help but think we're living in a Twilight Zone alternate universe where [The Onion](http://www.theonion.com/content/index) headlines are taking over. Does Prince really think that a simple home video threatens his rights and bank account? I could go on and on about each of the articles, pulling out quotes and explaining why the lawsuits seem frivolous and counter-productive to me, but I won't. People much smarter and more eloquent than I have gone on ad nauseam, and I mostly agree. That's not to say that these lawsuits are completely without merit. Lawyers are getting paid hundreds of thousands of dollars to protect valuable copyrights while I, with too much time on my hands, lob bricks at "the man". It's easy for me to sit here and say that a record label suing a woman for posting a video of her baby dancing to Prince is bullshit, and I'm not so naive as to think that these issues are as simple as I'm making them. Still, I can't help but think that this is wrong.

## Is the music industry too busy getting in its own way to move forward?

**Date:** Feb 23, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137323685/is-the-music-industry-too-busy-getting-in-its-own)

I'm an outspoken opponent of music piracy, and I have little doubt that it's played an important (and negative) role in the current state of the music industry. While I've occasionally been guilty of it in the past, I find that in most cases, excuses for piracy are generally guilt-driven maneuvers to make people feel better about themselves. I'm sure there are people who disagree, but in my opinion, if you like it, there's no reason you shouldn't buy it unless explicitly advocated by the band or label. That said, piracy isn't going anywhere, and arguments otherwise are naive and represent an unrealistic view of how the world works. Instead, the music industry needs to refocus their attention on providing fans with interesting, unique products. As a music fan, give me a compelling reason to spend money, and I'll gladly part with my cash. Few companies understand this better than [Topspin](http://www.topspinmedia.com/). According to [Hypebot](http://www.hypebot.com/hypebot/2010/02/topspin-shares-impressive-music-marketing-study.html), Topspin stated in a recent presentation that:

## The Two Sides of Hollywood

**Date:** Feb 19, 2010 [Original Post](https://blog.drewshannon.net/post/1137317047/the-two-sides-of-hollywood)

I'm not a film buff. I love movies, but I probably couldn't tell the difference between Truffaut and Fellini if my life depended on it. Nevertheless, the two "entertainment" news stories that most captured my interest last week were of the Hollywood variety. Circulating the Internet last week was a moving [Esquire feature about Roger Ebert](http://www.esquire.com/features/roger-ebert-0310), the Chicago Sun Times film critic whose cancer and subsequent surgeries have taken away his ability to eat, drink, or speak. Putting aside the fact that I had [a bout with lymphoma in college](http://laliydbta.blogspot.com/2008/04/its-over.html), I'm not sure why this article hit me as hard as it did, but it's stuck with me since reading it a few days ago. I think part of it is Ebert's grace, which is evident throughout the article.

The term is thrown around a lot, but I think Roger really gets it. He understands himself and the world around him, and while I generally view film critics as pretentious, he's become my new favorite person to follow on [Twitter](http://twitter.com/ebertchicago), with [witty](http://twitter.com/ebertchicago/status/9399123809) and [insightful](http://twitter.com/ebertchicago/status/9389352850) things to say. I never thought Ebert was the coolest guy around and regarded him as the epitome of cheeseball (two thumbs up\!), but this article, coupled with his new online personality has made him decidedly hip. On the other side of the coin is Kevin Smith, who was [kicked off a Southwest Airlines flight](http://silentbobspeaks.com/?p=394) earlier this week for reportedly being "too fat to fly". Kevin Smith is one of the rare people in entertainment whose personality I enjoy more than his work, and his weekly podcast, [SModcast](http://smodcast.com/), is the highlight of my week. I'm also a big fan of his "Evening With..." DVDs, which have been filmed during his famous 4-hour-long Q\&A sessions with fans. It's not that I dislike his movies, but I really like him as a person and find his point of view on things refreshing and anti-Hollywood. That's why, being the young left-leaning New Yorker that I am, I stood at the ready to defend him after the [tweet that was heard 'round the world](http://twitter.com/ThatKevinSmith/status/9079110598), lambasting Southwest Airlines for kicking him off the flight and starting his week-long campaign against the "man". Let it be clear that in no way do I support Southwest's behavior. From the way it sounds, they had no real reason to kick Smith off the plane, and they've handled things poorly. However, it's Kevin Smith's behavior that irritates me the most. In the days since, Smith's Twitter feed has been chock-full of tirades against Southwest and it's all that he's talked about. He recorded a special 1.5 hour episode of SModcast detailing the experience and once again calling out Southwest and how they handled the situation, and also recorded a 24-part (\!\!) series of YouTube clips explaining himself further. In these kinds of stories, I'm usually first in line to take huge corporations to task for shady business practices, but not here. Smith, whose humor often revolves around self-deprecating jokes about his weight, went from the guy who could laugh at himself, to the guy who asked \-- practically *begged* \-- for Southwest to publicly acknowledge that he wasn't "too fat to fly". Kevin Smith can call himself fat, but heaven forbid if someone calls him out on it. Mr. Smith, who is certainly overweight, will never be mistaken for thin, and while I applaud him for saying that his weight doesn't bother him, I think he's naive, and perhaps a bit selfish, to think that it wouldn't (or shouldn't) be a sticking point for someone else, let alone Southwest Airlines, a company in an industry known for consumer-unfriendly regulations and red tape. I'm not saying that I'll never see another Kevin Smith movie again, but his behavior this week has soured my opinion of him a little bit. [David Pell of Forbes seems to agree.](http://blogs.forbes.com/velocity/2010/02/18/kevin-smith-is-too-big-for-one-twitter-account/) One week, two articles, two reversals of opinion. Life can be surprising sometimes.
